. In other words, it is a solution that admits a
classical continuation to the interior
of
M
in terms of the classical equations of motion. In contrast,
J_{\partial M}\xi^{\mathrm{I}}
is the component that does not admit such a continuation. That is, its occurrence on the boundary
\partial M
is
classically forbidden
. Now, if the classically forbidden component
J_{\partial M}\xi^{\mathrm{I}}
is not present, i.e.,
\xi
is classically allowed, the amplitude (
3.24
) is simply unity. On the other hand, if
J_{\partial M}\xi^{\mathrm{I}}
is turned on, a phase factor appears, and more importantly, an exponential suppression term appears (recall that
g_{\partial M}
is positive-definite), depending on the magnitude of
J_{\partial M}\xi^{\mathrm{I}}
(or equivalently of
\xi^{\mathrm{I}}
). This is precisely the tunneling behavior expected in the quantum theory.

We remark that the inner product of coherent states can be seen to arise as a limiting case of the amplitude when the region
M
is being squeezed to a slice region
\hat{\Sigma}
, compare Axiom (T3x) of Appendix
B
. In that case the classically allowed solutions
L_{\hat{\Sigma}}\subseteq L_{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}
are the pairs
(\xi,\xi)
with
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}
. Then, the amplitude is the inner product of two identical normalized coherent states
\mathsf{k}_{\xi}
and thus unity. On the other hand, switching on a classically forbidden component means making the two solutions
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
on the two sides of
\hat{\Sigma}
different so that the inner product between the normalized coherent states becomes suppressed. Indeed, from expression (
3.13
) we obtain

\big\langle\mathsf{k}_{\xi_{1}},\mathsf{k}_{\xi_{2}}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=\exp\bigg(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{2},\xi_{1})-\frac{1}{4}g_{\Sigma}(\xi_{2}-\xi_{1},\xi_{2}-\xi_{1})\bigg).

3.4.2
Observables

Secondly, we consider correlation functions with observables. Let
\xi
be a
classically allowed solution in the sense that
\xi\in L_{M}
. For a Weyl observable
F
we have formula (
3.23
), where the first factor is unity and the last depends on
F
only (the vacuum correlation). The remaining factor is simply
F(\xi)
(observe
\hat{\xi}=\xi
here). That is, it is precisely the value of the classical observable
F
evaluated on the classically continued solution
\xi
in the interior of
M
. The semiclassical interpretation becomes even more clear when we switch to normal ordered quantization, formula (
3.27
). The vacuum correlation factor is removed and, more importantly, the classical value
F(\xi)
is obtained for arbitrary observables
F
. When the non-classical component
\xi^{\mathrm{I}}
is switched on the boundary, we no longer expect to obtain the value of the observable
F
on some classical solution. Indeed, in this case the solution
\hat{\xi}
in the interior becomes complex, a behavior well known in a tunneling context.

As for pure amplitudes, we can also in the case of observables consider the limit of squeezing a region to a slice region. This leads to the slice observables to be considered in Section
4
. The semiclassical interpretation in this limit will be considered in Section
5.1
.

3.5
Composition

As laid out in Section
1.3
, the notion of composition is central to GBQFT, as manifest in the composition rule for amplitudes (
1.4
). It turns out that to make this actually work with path integral quantization, a
gluing anomaly
factor has to be included that only depends on the geometry
[
35
]
. At the same time, it is convenient to formulate the composition rule in terms of the self-gluing of a region along matching pieces of its boundary. The composition of two distinct regions is then achieved by first performing a disjoint union, followed by a self-gluing.

We first describe the disjoint composition. Let
M
and
N
be regions and
\psi_{M}\in\mathcal{H}_{\partial M}
,
\psi_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{\partial N}
states on the boundaries. Let
F,G
be observables in
M
and
N
respectively. Then,

\rho_{M\sqcup N}^{F\bullet G}(\psi_{M}\otimes\psi_{N})=\rho_{M}^{F}(\psi_{M})\rho_{N}^{G}(\psi_{N}).

Here
F\bullet G
denotes the spacetime composition of the classical observables. This means the following.
F
and
G
are functions on the configuration spaces
K_{M}
and
K_{N}
respectively. We extend
F
to a function on
K_{M\sqcup N}=K_{M}\times K_{N}
trivially, i.e., without dependence on
K_{N}
, and proceed with
G
analogously. Then, we multiply the extended observables
F
and
G
as functions on
K_{M\sqcup N}
in the ordinary way. With this we satisfy Axiom (TO5a) of Appendix
B
.

Figure 8:
Self-composition of spacetime region with observable.

We turn to the self-composition. Let
M
be a region with its boundary decomposing as
\partial M=\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}
. Suppose that
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are identical so that
M
can be glued to itself along
\Sigma
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Call the resulting manifold
M_{1}
. Then,
\partial M_{1}=\Sigma_{1}
, see Figure
8
. In order for the composition rule to be valid, the gluing anomaly has to be well-defined.

Definition 3.2
.

We call the gluing
admissible
if the
gluing anomaly

c_{M;\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}
given by the following formula is well-defined due to absolute convergence of the sum,

c_{M;\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}=\sum_{k\in I}\rho_{M}\big(\mathsf{k}_{0}\otimes\zeta_{k}\otimes\iota_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\zeta_{k})\big).

Here,
\{\zeta_{k}\}_{k\in I}
is an arbitrary orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
.

Let
F\colon K_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
be an observable in
M
. Since field configurations in
M_{1}
yield field configurations in
M
, we have a map
K_{M_{1}}\to K_{M}
. By composition, we obtain from the observable
F
in
M
an observable
F_{1}\colon K_{M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
in
M_{1}
. The observables are also illustrated in Figure
8
. If the gluing is admissible, it can be shown that the correlation functions characterized by Theorem
3.1
satisfy the following composition rule
[
40
, Proposition 4.2]
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{\circ}_{\partial M_{1}}
. Then,

\rho_{M_{1}}^{F_{1}}(\psi)\cdot c_{M;\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}=\sum_{k\in I}\rho_{M}^{F}\big(\psi\otimes\zeta_{k}\otimes\iota_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\zeta_{k})\big).

Here,
\{\zeta_{k}\}_{k\in I}
is an arbitrary orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
.

\mathcal{H}^{\circ}_{\partial M_{1}}
denotes a dense subspace of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\partial M_{1}}
, where the amplitude map is well-defined. Here we take
\mathcal{H}^{\circ}_{\partial M_{1}}
to be the subspace spanned by the coherent states. With this we satisfy Axiom (TO5b) of Appendix
B
.

For the convenience of the reader we spell out the resulting composition rule for regions
M
and
N
, recall Figure
4
. Thus,
M
has boundary
\partial M=\Sigma_{M}\cup\Sigma
, and
N
has boundary
\partial N=\Sigma_{N}\cup\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}
. Here
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a copy of
\Sigma
. As before,
F
is an observable in
M
and
G
in
N
(not depicted in the figure). Then,

\rho_{M\cup N}^{F\bullet G}(\psi_{M}\otimes\psi_{N})\cdot c_{M,N;\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}=\sum_{k\in I}\rho_{M}^{F}(\psi_{M}\otimes\zeta_{k})\,\rho_{N}^{G}(\psi_{N}\otimes\iota_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\zeta_{k})).

(3.29)

This is the anomaly-corrected version of the composition rule (
1.4
).

4
Slice observables

4.1
Classical slice observables

In the present field theoretic setting, observables are functions on field configurations in spacetime regions. The idea of slice observables is simple: These are observables that only depend on the field in a
slice region

\hat{\Sigma}
, i.e., an infinitesimal neighborhood of a hypersurface
\Sigma
. In the case of equal-time hypersurfaces these observables are the analogues of the observables of non-relativistic quantum mechanics. As the latter, their quantized versions form algebras, in contrast to other observables in GBQFT.

D
\hat{\Sigma}
\Sigma
\Sigma^{\prime}
\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}
\eta_{\Sigma}
\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
\xi

Figure 9:
A slice observable
D
in the slice region
\hat{\Sigma}
.

Slice observables were introduced to GBQFT in
[
40
, Section 4.10]
. We recall the setup, with minor adjustments. Consider the slice region
\hat{\Sigma}
associated to the hypersurface
\Sigma
. We have the boundary decomposing into two copies of
\Sigma
, called
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
, with the second copy oppositely oriented,
\partial\hat{\Sigma}=\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}
, see Figure
9
. Note that
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are really “the same” hypersurface, i.e., “at the same place”. The separation drawn in Figure
9
is purely for purposes of illustration and intuition.
Recall that the space of solution in the interior of the slice region,
L_{\hat{\Sigma}}\subseteq L_{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}=L_{\Sigma}\oplus L_{\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}}
consists of those elements that take the form
(\phi,\phi)\in L_{\Sigma}\oplus L_{\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}}
. As for the space
K_{\hat{\Sigma}}
of field configurations, it turns out that the definition yielding the right properties is
K_{\hat{\Sigma}}\coloneqq L_{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}=L_{\Sigma}\oplus L_{\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}}
. That is, the field configurations are given by no more than the germs on the (two-component) boundary. However, we allow a slice observable
D\colon K_{\hat{\Sigma}}\to\mathbb{C}
only to depend on one copy of
L_{\Sigma}
. This ensures that it encodes the right degrees of freedom, achieving the desired correspondence to the non-relativistic setting. Concretely, the
slice observable

D
is determined by a map
D^{\prime}\colon L_{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{C}
.
D
takes on
(\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})\in K_{\hat{\Sigma}}
by definition the value

D((\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}}))\coloneqq D^{\prime}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}(\phi_{\Sigma}+\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})\bigg).

(4.1)

One can think of this as an “averaging of the boundary values”. Another way to think about this is in terms of a decomposition
L_{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}=L_{\hat{\Sigma}}\oplus L_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{\rm c}
into solutions in the interior,
L_{\hat{\Sigma}}
, and a complementary space
L_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{\rm c}
of elements of the form
(\phi,-\phi)
. The “averaging” in formula (
4.1
) is then really a projection onto the subspace
L_{\hat{\Sigma}}\subseteq L_{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}
with the effect that
D
only depends on this subspace.

If the slice observable
D
is real
linear
we can encode it through an element
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}
such that, for all
\phi\in L_{\Sigma}
,
17
17

17

There is a difference in convention compared to equation (104) in
[
40
]
corresponding to a relative minus sign.

D^{\prime}(\phi)=2\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi,\phi).

(4.2)

Let this slice observable now act as a source, localized in
\hat{\Sigma}
. That is, we modify the action
S
by adding
D
to obtain
S+D
. As before, we call the affine space of solutions of the modified equations of motion
A_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{D}
. It is useful to think of these solutions as extending to the ambient spacetime around
\hat{\Sigma}
. Now, since the source is singular in the sense of being concentrated on a hypersurface, so will be the solutions. In fact, using the relation (
2.5
) we can calculate exactly how a solution “jumps” from one side of
\hat{\Sigma}
to the other. On each side of
\hat{\Sigma}
a solution
\eta\in A_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{D}
behaves as a solution of the homogeneous equations of motion corresponding to the free action
S
. Call these partial solutions
\eta_{\Sigma}
and
\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
respectively. Formally we treat these as elements of
L_{\Sigma}
.

Let
\phi\in L_{\Sigma}
be arbitrary. Then
(\phi,\phi)\in L_{\hat{\Sigma}}\subseteq K_{\hat{\Sigma}}
. Evaluating the observable
D
on
(\phi,\phi)
according to (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) yields

D((\phi,\phi))=D^{\prime}(\phi)=2\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi,\phi).

On the other hand, formula (
2.5
) is equally applicable in this case and leads to

D((\phi,\phi))=2\omega_{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}\left((\phi,\phi),(\eta_{\Sigma},\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})\right)=2\omega_{\Sigma}(\phi,\eta_{\Sigma})+2\omega_{\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}}(\phi,\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})=2\omega_{\Sigma}(\phi,\eta_{\Sigma}-\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}}).

Since
\phi
was arbitrary we can conclude that
\eta\in A_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{D}
, i.e.,
\eta
is a solution of the inhomogeneous equations of motion iff

\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}}-\eta_{\Sigma}=\xi.

(4.3)

The “jump” is precisely given by
\xi
. This is also illustrated in Figure
9
. A particular choice of solution is given by

\eta=(\eta_{\Sigma},\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})=\bigg({-}\frac{1}{2}\xi,\frac{1}{2}\xi\bigg).

Analogous to the previous discussion in Section
2.1
, once we have the result (
4.3
) we drop the restriction to the real case. That is, we allow
D
to be complex valued (on real solutions) and correspondingly
\xi
to be complex, i.e.,
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
, extending the applicability of formula (
4.3
). Moreover, we require
D
to be defined and holomorphic on the complexified configuration space by requiring
D^{\prime}
to be defined and holomorphic on
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

As an example, it turns out that the field value at a point, besides defining an ordinary (spacetime) observable (as in Section
1.6
), can also be implemented as a slice observable. We make this concrete in the previously considered case of Klein–Gordon theory on an equal-time hypersurface
\Sigma_{t}
in Minkowski space. Thus fix
x\in\Sigma_{t}
and in terms of the mode expansion (
3.18
) let
18
18

18

Strictly speaking the solution
\xi
is not normalizable in the space of germs of solutions viewed as a Hilbert space. To remedy this one would typically smear it with source functions in spacetime.

\displaystyle\xi^{\rm a}(k)=\xi^{{\rm b}}(k)=\mathrm{i}\exp(\mathrm{i}(Et-kx)).

(4.4)

Then, define the corresponding linear observable
D\colon K_{\hat{\Sigma}_{t}}\to\mathbb{R}
by equation (
4.2
). With the explicit form of the symplectic structure (
3.20
) we find

D^{\prime}(\phi)=2\omega_{\Sigma_{t}}(\xi,\phi)=\phi(t,x).

(4.5)

4.2
Quantum algebra of slice observables

\Sigma_{1}
\Sigma_{2}
\Sigma_{3}
\eta_{\Sigma_{1}}
\eta_{\Sigma_{3}}
\xi_{D}
\xi_{E}
G=\exp(\mathrm{i}E)
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
\eta_{\Sigma_{2}}

Figure 10:
Composition of Weyl slice observables
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
and
G=\exp(\mathrm{i}E)
.

Classically, slice observables obviously form a commutative algebra, simply by viewing them as holomorphic maps
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}

\big(
or equivalently induced maps
K_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}\big)
. In the quantum theory the only notion of composition is mediated by spacetime composition. A slice region
\hat{\Sigma}
can be glued to a copy of itself, resulting in the very same slice region. Thus, a slice observable can be composed with another slice observable defined on the same slice region. What is more, we shall show that the resulting object, which is again associated to the same slice region, is in fact again a slice observable. That is, upon quantization, the slice observables form an algebra. The product is non-commutative with the order reflecting the spatio-temporal arrangement of the gluing process, see Figure
10
. In the particular case of equal-time hypersurfaces, we recover the product reflecting the temporal order of observables, as in non-relativistic quantum mechanics.

The quantization of observables is ultimately governed by formula (
2.6
) and its implicit generalization for non-Weyl observables. Thus, to show that the composition of two slice observables is a specific slice observable, we have to show that inserting the former observables into this formula yields the exact same results as inserting the latter observable. This has to be true not only in the presence of any vacuum (i.e., any polarization on the boundary) but also in the presence of arbitrary other observables, as long as they are localized outside the slice region
\hat{\Sigma}
. Due to their generating nature, it is sufficient to consider this for the case that all observables are Weyl observables.

Thus, consider two copies of the slice region
\hat{\Sigma}
, glued to form another copy, see Figure
10
. To keep track of the involved hypersurfaces (all copies of
\Sigma
, but with infinitesimal transversal displacement) we label them
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{3}
. Thus, the first component slice region has boundary
\Sigma_{1}\cup\overline{\Sigma_{2}}
and the second one
\Sigma_{2}\cup\overline{\Sigma_{3}}
. Now consider linear maps
D^{\prime}\colon L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
and
E^{\prime}\colon L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
that determine linear slice observables
D\colon L_{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
and
E\colon L_{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
via formula (
4.1
). We denote the corresponding elements of
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
via formula (
4.2
) by
\xi_{D}
and
\xi_{E}
respectively. Also, let
F\coloneqq\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
and
G\coloneqq\exp(\mathrm{i}E)
denote the corresponding Weyl observables. We locate
F
between
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
, and
G
between
\Sigma_{2}
and
\Sigma_{3}
. In line with our previous use of this notation we write
G\bullet F
for the spacetime composite of the Weyl observables on
K_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}\cup K_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}
. However, we will wish to identify
K_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}\cup K_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}
with
K_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}
itself, in line with the gluing identity
\hat{\Sigma}\cup\hat{\Sigma}=\hat{\Sigma}
for the underlying slice regions.

Consider now a solution
\eta
in a neighborhood of
\hat{\Sigma}
, of the equations of motion modified by adding
D
and
E
to the action. The analogue of equation (
4.3
) is now given by two corresponding equations, with obvious notation,

\eta_{\Sigma_{2}}-\eta_{\Sigma_{1}}=\xi_{D},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\eta_{\Sigma_{3}}-\eta_{\Sigma_{2}}=\xi_{E}.

(4.6)

An immediate consequence is

\eta_{\Sigma_{3}}-\eta_{\Sigma_{1}}=\xi_{D}+\xi_{E}=\xi_{D+E}.

(4.7)

We proceed to evaluate the analogue of the right-hand side of expression (
2.6
) using formula (
4.1
) for each of
D
and
E
. Crucially, the following computation arises as a factor in any evaluation of (
2.6
), irrespective of the boundary polarization and any additional Weyl observables present outside
\hat{\Sigma}
,

\displaystyle\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(E+D)(\eta)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\bigg(E^{\prime}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}(\eta_{\Sigma_{2}}+\eta_{\Sigma_{3}})\bigg)+D^{\prime}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}(\eta_{\Sigma_{1}}+\eta_{\Sigma_{2}})\bigg)\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{E},\eta_{\Sigma_{2}})+\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{D},\eta_{\Sigma_{2}})\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{E}+\xi_{D},\eta_{\Sigma_{2}})\big).

Substituting in the last expression
\eta_{\Sigma_{2}}
with
\frac{1}{2}\big(\xi_{D}+\eta_{\Sigma_{1}}+\eta_{\Sigma_{3}}-\xi_{E}\big)
, a consequence of (
4.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(E+D)(\eta)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{E},\xi_{D}))\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\xi_{E}+\xi_{D},\eta_{\Sigma_{1}}+\eta_{\Sigma_{3}}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{E},\xi_{D}))\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(E^{\prime}+D^{\prime})\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\big(\eta_{\Sigma_{1}}+\eta_{\Sigma_{3}}\big)\bigg)\bigg).

We notice that the resulting expression does not contain any explicit reference to the “intermediate” value
\eta_{\Sigma_{2}}
. In particular, we may consistently set
K_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}\cup K_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}=K_{\hat{\Sigma}}=L_{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}
, as desired. Furthermore, the resulting dependence on the values
\eta_{\Sigma_{1}}
and
\eta_{\Sigma_{3}}
on the boundary of
\hat{\Sigma}
is exactly as in formula (
4.1
). That is, the composite
G\bullet F
of the Weyl slice observables, upon quantization, i.e., insertion into formula (
2.6
) behaves as a single slice observable that we shall call
G\star F
. In fact, it behaves even as a Weyl slice observable, up to a phase factor. Taking also into account relation (
4.7
) we note that this resulting Weyl slice observable coincides with the quantization of the classical product of
G
and
F
, i.e., with
G\cdot F
, up to the phase factor. In formal notation,

G\star F=\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{E},\xi_{D})\right)G\cdot F.

(4.8)

It should not come as a surprise that the relations we obtain are precisely the
Weyl relations
. In particular, the complex vector space spanned by the Weyl observables becomes a non-commutative algebra in this way. This algebra structure extends to all other slice observables that we can generate by differentiating with respect to linear observables.
The new product on the elements of this algebra viewed as functions on the phase space
L_{\Sigma}
is also precisely the
Groenewold–Moyal product

[
19
,
26
]
.
We denote the quantum algebra of slice observables associated to a hypersurface
\Sigma
by
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}
.

4.3
Vacuum correlation function of a Weyl slice observable

The vacuum correlation function for a single Weyl slice observable in a slice region is easily obtained. Due to its importance for later considerations, we exhibit it explicitly. We assume the same setting as in Section
4.1
in terms of a hypersurface
\Sigma
, the corresponding slice region
\hat{\Sigma}
(recall Figure
9
), as well as a linear slice observable
D
determined by a complex linear map
D^{\prime}\colon L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
in terms of equation (
4.1
), with the latter corresponding to
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
via equation (
4.2
). Let
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
be the corresponding Weyl slice observable. We assume the vacua on the two sides (
\Sigma
and
\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}
) of the slice region
\hat{\Sigma}
given in terms of transversal polarizations
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
and
L_{\Sigma}^{\overline{\rm P}}
. That is, the space
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
of complexified germs of solutions on
\Sigma
decomposes as a direct sum of Lagrangian subspaces
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}=L_{\Sigma}^{{\rm P}}\oplus L_{\Sigma}^{{\overline{\rm P}}}
. In terms of elements we write this decomposition as
\xi=\xi^{\rm P}+\xi^{\overline{\rm P}}
.

To evaluate formula (
2.6
) for the vacuum correlation function we need to find the solution
\eta\in A_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{D,\mathbb{C}}
of the inhomogeneous equations of motion determined by
D
satisfying the boundary conditions, recall Section
4.1
. The latter are given by the transversal polarizations
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
and
L_{\Sigma}^{\overline{\rm P}}
, i.e.,
\eta_{\Sigma}\in L^{\rm P}_{\Sigma}
and
\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\in L^{\overline{\rm P}}_{\Sigma}
. With relation (
4.3
) we obtain,
\eta_{\Sigma}=-\xi^{\rm P}
and
\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}}=\xi^{\overline{\rm P}}
. We may now evaluate (
2.6
) to get

\displaystyle\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta_{\Sigma},\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})\bigg)=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D^{\prime}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}(\eta_{\Sigma}+\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\xi,\xi^{\overline{\rm P}}-\xi^{\rm P}\big)\bigg)=\exp\big({-}\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\xi^{\overline{\rm P}},\xi^{\rm P}\big)\big).

(4.9)

In the case that
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
is a Kähler polarization
L_{\Sigma}^{+}
with conjugate
L_{\Sigma}^{\overline{\rm P}}=L_{\Sigma}^{-}
we can rewrite this in terms of the inner product (
3.2
) by using the explicit form (
3.4
) of the projectors onto the Lagrangian subspaces,

\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

(4.10)

4.4
Vacuum correlation functions of quadratic observables

In order to illustrate the versatility of slice observables we show in the following how they can be used to derive vacuum correlation functions of quadratic observables. We also consider the concrete case of the Feynman propagator of Klein–Gordon quantum field theory.

We are interested in the product observable
D=D_{1}\cdot D_{2}
of the linear observables
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
. Its vacuum correlation function is given by formula (
2.9
). To evaluate it, we need to find the inhomogeneous solutions
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
of the equations of motion modified by
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
respectively and satisfying the boundary conditions corresponding to the vacuum. To this end we shall assume that
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are supported on disjoint spacetime regions, separated by a hypersurface
\Sigma
. For ease of language we shall refer to the side of
\Sigma
, where
D_{1}
is supported as the “past” and the side where
D_{2}
is supported as the “future”. This suggests that
\Sigma
is a spacelike hypersurface, although this might not be the case. We shall take the orientation of
\Sigma
to correspond to its “past” side while that of its “future” side is given by
\overline{\Sigma}
. Let the pair
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
and
L_{\Sigma}^{\overline{\rm P}}
of transversal Lagrangian subspaces of
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
determine the vacuum in the “past” and “future” of
\Sigma
respectively.

For the moment we assume furthermore that
D_{1}
is given by a slice observable on a hypersurface
\Sigma_{1}
to the past of
\Sigma
and such that the spaces
L_{\Sigma}
and
L_{\Sigma_{1}}
of germs of solutions are in one-to-one correspondence as are the vacua. We also make the corresponding assumption for
D_{2}
. Thus, there are elements
\xi_{i}\in L_{\Sigma_{i}}^{\mathbb{C}}
determining the observables
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
via equations (
4.1
) and (
4.2
). The solution
\eta_{1}
then consists of an “early” and “late time” part
\eta_{1,<}
and
\eta_{1,>}
, “before” and “after” hypersurface
\Sigma_{1}
respectively. Their relation as homogeneous solutions is given by
\eta_{1,>}=\eta_{1,<}+\xi_{1}
, compare Section
4.1
. Moreover, the boundary conditions are given by,
\eta_{1,<}\in L_{\Sigma_{1}}^{{\rm P}}
and
\eta_{1,>}\in L_{\Sigma_{1}}^{\overline{\rm P}}
. Thus,
\eta_{1,<}=-\xi_{1}^{\rm P}
and
\eta_{1,>}=\xi_{1}^{\overline{\rm P}}
. The considerations for solution
\eta_{2}
are analogous, and we obtain,
\eta_{2,<}=-\xi_{2}^{\rm P}
and
\eta_{2,>}=\xi_{2}^{\overline{\rm P}}
. At this point we may notice that we can identify all the elements
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}
,
\xi_{1}
,
\xi_{2}
as well as their polarized components with corresponding elements of
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
, and shall do so without modifying notation. In this way the locations of the hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
become irrelevant. Due to linearity we may then replace
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
with linear combinations of slice observables located on different hypersurfaces and thus with arbitrary linear observables (still located respectively in the “past” and “future” of
\Sigma
, however). We obtain for the vacuum correlation function

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{D_{1}D_{2}}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\big(D_{1}(\eta_{2,<})+D_{2}(\eta_{1,>})\big)=-\mathrm{i}\Big({-}\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\rm P}\big)+\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\xi_{2},\xi_{1}^{\overline{\rm P}}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\xi_{1}^{\overline{\rm P}},\xi_{2}^{\rm P}\big).

(4.11)

We now assume in addition that the polarizations are conjugate Kähler polarizations,
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}=L_{\Sigma}^{+}
and
L_{\Sigma}^{\overline{\rm P}}=L_{\Sigma}^{-}
.
Then, in order to re-express
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
in terms of
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
we may use an orthonormal basis
\{u_{k}\}_{k\in I}
of
L_{\Sigma}^{+}
with respect to the inner product (
1.6
). Thus,

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{D_{1}D_{2}}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}2\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{1}^{-},u_{k})\,\big(u_{k},\xi_{2}^{+}\big)_{\Sigma}=\sum_{k}2\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{1}^{-},u_{k})\,4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\overline{u}_{k},\xi_{2}^{+}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}2\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{1},u_{k})\,4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\overline{u}_{k},\xi_{2})=2\sum_{k}D_{1}(u_{k})D_{2}(\overline{u}_{k}).

This recovers and generalizes well known formulas from the literature, such as DeWitt’s vacuum correlation function for the energy-momentum tensor
[
11
]
.
Alternatively, we may use other completeness relations. Using (
3.14
) we obtain

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{D_{1}D_{2}}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\{\xi_{1}^{-},\xi_{2}^{+}\}_{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}}\{\xi_{1}^{-},\phi\}_{\Sigma}\{\phi,\xi_{2}^{+}\}_{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\phi)

\displaystyle=-\!\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}}\!\!2\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{1}^{-},\phi^{+})\,2\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\phi^{-},\xi_{2}^{+}\big)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\phi)=\!\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}}\!\!2\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{1},\phi^{+})\,2\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{2},\phi^{-})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}}D_{1}(\phi^{+})\,D_{2}(\phi^{-})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\phi).

We proceed to focus on the particular quadratic observable given by the two-point function of Klein–Gordon theory in Minkowski spacetime. Consider spacetime points
(t_{1},x_{1})
and
(t_{2},x_{2})
and suppose
t_{2}>t_{1}
. We set up linear observables
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
such that

D_{i}(\phi)=\phi(t_{i},x_{i}).

For the hypersurface
\Sigma
we may take any equal-time hypersurface at time
t
with
t_{1}<t<t_{2}
. By comparison with equation (
4.5
) we find that the elements
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in L_{\Sigma}
determining the observables
D_{1}
,
D_{2}
take the form (
4.4
).
Inserting this into expression (
4.11
) we obtain the familiar 2-point function and Feynman propagator (for
t_{1}<t_{2}
),

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{D_{1}D_{2}}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}G_{F}((t_{1},x_{1}),(t_{2},x_{2}))=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}2E}\,\xi_{1}(k)\overline{\xi_{2}(k)}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}2E}\,{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}(E(t_{1}-t_{2})-k(x_{1}-x_{2}))}.

Removing the constraint on the time ordering of arguments, we have the usual formula,

\displaystyle G_{F}((t_{1},x_{1}),(t_{2},x_{2}))

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\mathrm{i}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}2E}\,\big(\theta(t_{2}-t_{1}){\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}(E(t_{1}-t_{2})-k(x_{1}-x_{2}))}+\theta(t_{1}-t_{2}){\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}(E(t_{2}-t_{1})-k(x_{2}-x_{1}))}\big).

5
Slice observables in Kähler quantization

5.1
Quantized slice observables as operators

In GBQFT, observables give rise to correlation functions, but in general not to operators on some particular space. In the Kähler quantization setting (Section
3
), the slice observables are an exception. Consider a hypersurface
\Sigma
with associated space of germs
L_{\Sigma}
, symplectic form
\omega_{\Sigma}
, and Kähler polarization
L_{\Sigma}^{+}\subseteq L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Denote by
\hat{\Sigma}
the slice region associated to
\Sigma
, with boundary
\partial\hat{\Sigma}=\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma}
. We recall the relation between the inner product on
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
and the amplitude map
\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}
(Axiom (T3x) in Appendix
B
),

\langle\eta,\psi\rangle_{\Sigma}=\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}(\psi\otimes\iota_{\Sigma}(\eta)).

(5.1)

An observable
F\colon K_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}=L_{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
determines an operator
\hat{F}
on the state space
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
via

\big\langle\eta,\hat{F}\psi\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{F}(\psi\otimes\iota_{\Sigma}(\eta)).

(5.2)

Recall Figure
9
for an illustration.

Crucially, this definition brings into exact correspondence the composition of so defined operators with the spacetime composition of the underlying slice observables, compare Figure
10
. That is

\displaystyle\big\langle\eta,\hat{G}\hat{F}\psi\big\rangle_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in I}\big\langle\eta,\hat{G}\zeta_{k}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}\big\langle\zeta_{k},\hat{F}\psi\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=\sum_{k\in I}\rho^{G}_{\hat{\Sigma}}(\zeta_{k}\otimes\iota_{\Sigma}(\eta))\rho^{F}_{\hat{\Sigma}}(\psi\otimes\iota_{\Sigma}(\zeta_{k}))

\displaystyle=\rho^{G\bullet F}_{\hat{\Sigma}}(\psi\otimes\iota_{\Sigma}(\eta)).

(5.3)

The last equality is due to Theorem
3.3
, in the form of equation (
3.29
), with no anomaly present in this case, i.e.,
c=1
. As before, we have adopted the notation
G\bullet F
for the spacetime composite of the slice observables
G
and
F
, arranged as in Figure
10
. Again,
\{\zeta_{k}\}_{k\in I}
denotes an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
.

Suppose that
F
is a Weyl slice observable
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
with
D
linear and thus determined by equation (
4.1
) from a linear map
D^{\prime}\colon L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
. We let
D^{\prime}
be determined by an element
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
according to equation (
4.2
).

Proposition 5.1
.

The operator
\hat{F}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
acts on coherent states as

\big(\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big)(\phi)=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\{\xi,\phi\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\xi\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

(5.4)

Proof.

By equations (
3.12
), (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) as well as equations (
3.23
) and (
3.25
) of Theorem
3.1
we have

\big(\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big)(\phi)=\big\langle\mathsf{K}_{\phi},\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{F}(\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\otimes\mathsf{K}_{\phi})=\langle\mathsf{K}_{\phi},\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\rangle_{\Sigma}F\big(\hat{(\tau,\phi)}\big)\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta)\bigg).

(Note that in our implicit notation
\iota_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{K}_{\phi})=\mathsf{K}_{\phi}
.)
Here,
\eta\in A_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{D,\mathbb{C}}\cap L_{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}^{+}
is unique and the last factor is given by equation (
4.10
), compare Section
4.3
,

\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta)\bigg)=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

The first factor is the inner product (
3.13
), here,

\langle\mathsf{K}_{\phi},\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\rangle_{\Sigma}=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

(5.5)

As for the remaining factor, we note

\hat{(\tau,\phi)}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\tau+\phi-\mathrm{i}J_{\Sigma}(\phi-\tau)\right).

With equation (
4.2
) we get

F\big(\hat{(\tau,\phi)}\big)=\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\Sigma}\left(\xi,\tau+\phi-\mathrm{i}J_{\Sigma}(\phi-\tau)\right)\right).

With the definition (
3.2
) of the inner product we obtain

F\big(\hat{(\tau,\phi)}\big)=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\xi,\phi\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

(5.6)

Joining the factors yields the claimed identity.
∎

As is easily seen, this satisfies the Weyl relation (
4.8
) as follows already from (
5.3
) when comparing with the results of Section
4.2
. This fact was also shown previously in
[
40
, Proposition 4.5]
.
19
19

19

There, more general observables where considered which results in extra terms that are absent here. Also note differences in ordering conventions.

Since Weyl observables are generators for general observables and coherent states are generators for general states, Proposition
5.1
completely characterizes the action of slice observables on states.

The classical algebra of slice observables (see Section
4.1
) carries a natural
*
-structure. If we consider such an observable
F
as merely living on the real configuration space
K_{\Sigma}
, i.e., as a map
F\colon K_{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{C}
, then the
*
-operation is simply complex conjugation. However, as explained in Section
2.1
we generally consider observables as holomorphic functions on the complexification of the configuration space, here
F\colon K_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
. The natural extension of the notion of complex conjugation to this setting is given by

F^{*}(\phi)\coloneqq\overline{F\big(\overline{\phi}\big)}.

(5.7)

In this way,
F^{*}
is again holomorphic. It is now easy to verify from (
4.8
) (and a well-known fact) that this
*
-structure is also compatible with the product of the quantum algebra of slice observables (Section
4.2
). That is,
(G\star F)^{*}=F^{*}\star G^{*}
. We may verify moreover, that it translates precisely to taking the adjoint of the corresponding operator in the sense of relation (
5.2
).

Proposition 5.2
.

For an arbitrary slice observable
F
,
\hat{F^{*}}=\hat{F}^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

As coherent states span a dense subspace, it is sufficient to show this for matrix elements of coherent states. That is, it is sufficient to show

\big\langle\mathsf{K}_{\phi},\hat{F}^{\dagger}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=\big\langle\mathsf{K}_{\phi},\hat{F^{*}}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}.

Moreover, since Weyl observables are generating observables it is sufficient to show this for
F
a Weyl observable. Defining
F
as above, using the reproducing property (
3.12
) and Proposition
5.1
we get for the left-hand side

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathsf{K}_{\phi},\hat{F}^{\dagger}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\big\langle\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{\phi},\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=\overline{\big\langle\mathsf{K}_{\tau},\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{\phi}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}}

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\overline{\{\phi,\tau\}_{\Sigma}}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\{\xi,\tau\}_{\Sigma}}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\{\phi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}}-\frac{1}{4}\overline{\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\overline{\xi}\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\{\overline{\xi},\phi\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\big\{\overline{\xi},\overline{\xi}\big\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

On the other hand, as is easy to see, replacing
F
with
F^{*}
amounts to replacing
D
by
-D^{*}
and thus
\xi
by
-\overline{\xi}
. So, again using (
3.12
) and Proposition
5.1
we get for the right-hand side

\big\langle\mathsf{K}_{\phi},\hat{F^{*}}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\big\{{-}\overline{\xi},\phi\big\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\big\{\tau,-\overline{\xi}\big\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\big\{{-}\overline{\xi},-\overline{\xi}\big\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

It remains to read off the coincidence between the two sides.
∎

We return to the case that
F
is a Weyl observable determined by the linear observable
D
, in turn determined by
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Important special cases for
\xi
in certain subspaces are given by the following Corollary of Proposition
5.1
.

Corollary 5.3
.

If
\xi
is real, i.e.,
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}
we obtain the unitary action

\hat{F}\mathsf{k}_{\tau}=\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi,\tau)\right)\mathsf{k}_{\tau+\xi}.

(5.8)

If
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{-}
we get

\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}=\mathsf{K}_{\tau+\xi+\overline{\xi}}.

(5.9)

Proof.

If
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}
, we recognize the first two terms in the exponential on the right-hand side of (
5.4
) as the wave function (
3.10
) of the coherent state
\mathsf{K}_{\xi+\tau}
. This yields,

\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\xi\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)\mathsf{K}_{\tau+\xi}.

Substituting ordinary coherent states with normalized coherent states with the respective normalization factors, recall (
3.13
), leads to (
5.8
).

If
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{-}
, then the third and fourth term in the exponential on the right-hand side of (
5.4
) vanish. This yields,

\big(\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big)(\phi)=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\{\xi,\phi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\big\{\tau+\xi+\overline{\xi},\phi\big\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

This is the desired expression (
5.9
) in terms of wave functions.
∎

The last part of the Corollary, manifest in relation (
5.9
), can be generalized considerably as follows. This follows from the fact that observables holomorphic with respect to
J_{\Sigma}
are generated by Weyl observables holomorphic with respect to
J_{\Sigma}
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Suppose the slice observable
F
is holomorphic with respect to
J_{\Sigma}

(
invariant under translation in
L_{\Sigma}^{-}
, i.e.,
F(\phi)=F(\phi+\xi)
for all
\phi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
and
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{-})
. Then,
\hat{F}
acts by multiplication of wave functions,

\big(\hat{F}\psi\big)(\phi)=F(\phi)\psi(\phi).

(5.10)

Proof.

We assume at first that
F
is a Weyl observable with
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{-}
. Then, we can rewrite expression (
5.9
) as,

\big(\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big)(\phi)=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\xi,\phi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)\mathsf{K}_{\tau}(\phi)=\exp\left(2\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi,\phi)\right)\mathsf{K}_{\tau}(\phi)=F(\phi)\mathsf{K}_{\tau}(\phi).

Since the coherent states span a dense subspace, the corresponding equality extends to all states. We may then extend the obtained equality (
5.10
) to more general
J_{\Sigma}
-holomorphic observables by derivative methods analogous to those exhibited in Section
2.2
. On the other hand, we may take advantage of
J_{\Sigma}
-holomorphicity to treat the observable
F
as if it was a wave function. (This requires square-integrability of
F
with respect to the measure
\nu_{\Sigma}
, recall Section
3.1
.) In particular, using the completeness relation (
3.14
) for coherent states and the reproducing property (
3.12
) we obtain an integral representation of
F
in terms of Weyl observables. To this end we shall denote the Weyl observable determined by
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
by
G_{\xi}
. We have for
\phi\in L_{\Sigma}
,

\displaystyle F(\phi)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathsf{K}_{\phi},F\rangle_{\Sigma}=\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}}\langle\mathsf{K}_{\phi},\mathsf{K}_{\xi}\rangle_{\Sigma}\langle\mathsf{K}_{\xi},F\rangle_{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\xi)=\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}}\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\xi,\phi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)F(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}}\exp\big(2\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(P^{-}(\xi),\phi)\big)F(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\xi)=\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}}G_{P^{-}(\xi)}(\phi)F(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\xi).

Inserting this integral representation in the quantization map, yields, in terms of wave functions

\displaystyle\big(\hat{F}\psi\big)(\phi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}}\left(\hat{G}_{P^{-}(\xi)}\psi\right)(\phi)F(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\xi)=\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}}G_{P^{-}(\xi)}(\phi)\psi(\phi)F(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\xi)

\displaystyle=F(\phi)\psi(\phi).

∎

On the other hand, we may specialize to the case that we act on the vacuum, yielding another Corollary of Proposition
5.4
.

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
\nu\coloneqq P^{-}(\xi)+\overline{P^{-}(\xi)}
. Then,

\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{0}=\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi,\nu)\right)\mathsf{k}_{\nu}.

(5.11)

Proof.

If
\tau=0
the first and third term on the right-hand side of (
5.4
) vanish. On the other hand, we notice,
\{\xi,\phi\}_{\Sigma}=\{P^{-}(\xi),\phi\}_{\Sigma}=\big\{P^{-}(\xi)+\overline{P^{-}(\xi)},\phi\big\}_{\Sigma}=\{\nu,\phi\}_{\Sigma}
. This yields

\displaystyle\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{0}

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)\mathsf{K}_{\nu}=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{1}{4}\{\nu,\nu\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)\mathsf{k}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\nu,\xi\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\nu\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)\mathsf{k}_{\nu}.

The exponential term can be rewritten to yield the desired equality (
5.11
).
∎

We also consider the action of linear slice observables, here
D
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\xi^{\rm c}\coloneqq P^{-}(\xi)+\overline{P^{-}(\xi)}
and
\xi^{\rm a}\coloneqq P^{+}(\xi)+\overline{P^{+}(\xi)}
. The operator
\hat{D}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
acts as

\hat{D}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{2}}\big(a_{\xi^{\rm a}}-a^{\dagger}_{\xi^{\rm c}}\big).

(5.12)

Proof.

Taking a derivative of expression (
5.4
) as in relation (
2.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\hat{D}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big)(\phi)

\displaystyle=(-\mathrm{i})\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda\{\xi,\phi\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\{\tau,\xi\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\lambda^{2}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)\bigg|_{\lambda=0}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\xi,\phi\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\{\tau,\xi\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\{\xi,\phi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\{\tau,\xi^{\rm a}\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\{\xi^{\rm c},\phi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{2}}\big(a_{\xi^{\rm a}}-a^{\dagger}_{\xi^{\rm c}}\big)\mathsf{K}_{\tau}(\phi).

Here we have used expressions (
3.8
) and (
3.11
) for the action of creation and annihilation operators. It remains to observe that by linearity and by denseness of the coherent states this equality applies to all states, yielding (
5.12
).
∎

It is useful to define linear slice observables that upon quantization turn into a given creation or annihilation operator. For
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}
we define the slice observables
A^{\dagger}_{\xi}
and
A_{\xi}
on
\Sigma
via

{A_{\xi}^{\dagger}}^{\prime}(\phi)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\{\xi,\phi\}_{\Sigma},\qquad A_{\xi}^{\prime}(\phi)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\{\phi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}.

With Proposition
5.6
this yields

\hat{A}^{\dagger}_{\xi}=a^{\dagger}_{\xi},\qquad\hat{A}_{\xi}=a_{\xi}.

In the example of Klein–Gordon theory in Minkowski space on an equal-time hypersurface
\Sigma_{t}
(recall Section
3.2
), the observables
A_{p}^{\dagger}
and
A_{p}
yielding the creation and annihilation operators
a_{p}^{\dagger}
and
a_{p}
for the momentum modes thus take the form

{A_{p}^{\dagger}}^{\prime}(\phi)=\overline{\phi^{{\rm b}}(p)},\qquad A_{p}^{\prime}(\phi)=\phi^{\rm a}(p).

We proceed to consider normal ordered quantization as defined by formula (
3.27
) for the case of slice observables. In particular, this gives justification to the name “normal ordered” as we recover the usual normal ordering of operators.

Proposition 5.7
.

The operator
{:}\hat{F}{:}
acts on coherent states as

\big({:}\hat{F}{:}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big)(\phi)=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\{\xi,\phi\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

(5.13)

Proof.

With the reproducing property (
3.12
), the normal ordered version of relation (
5.2
), and the definition (
3.27
) we have

\big({:}\hat{F}{:}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big)(\phi)=\big\langle\mathsf{K}_{\phi},{:}\hat{F}{:}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{{:}F{:}}(\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\otimes\mathsf{K}_{\phi})=\langle\mathsf{K}_{\phi},\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\rangle_{\Sigma}F\big(\hat{(\phi,\tau)}\big).

We now recall that we have evaluated the two factors on the right-hand side already in the proof of Proposition
5.1
, see equations (
5.5
) and (
5.6
). This recovers the result (
5.13
).
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n},\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{m}\in L_{\Sigma}
. Let
E=A_{\xi_{1}}\cdots A_{\xi_{n}}\cdot A_{\eta_{1}}^{\dagger}\cdots A_{\eta_{m}}^{\dagger}
. Then,

{:}\hat{E}{:}=a_{\eta_{1}}^{\dagger}\cdots a_{\eta_{m}}^{\dagger}a_{\xi_{1}}\cdots a_{\xi_{n}}.

Proof.

We proceed as at the beginning of Section
2.2
to deal with a product of linear observables. Thus, define

\displaystyle E_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}}\coloneqq\lambda_{1}A_{\xi_{1}}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}A_{\xi_{n}}+\mu_{1}A_{\eta_{1}}^{\dagger}\cdots\mu_{m}A_{\eta_{m}},

\displaystyle F_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}}\coloneqq\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,E_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}}\big).

Then, as in relation (
2.7
),

E=(-\mathrm{i})^{n+m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{1}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{1}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{m}}F_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}}\bigg|_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}=0}.

(5.14)

If
\xi_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}}\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
is related to
E_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}}
as in equation (
4.2
), then,

\xi_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}}=\sqrt{2}\mathrm{i}\big({-}\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}^{+}-\cdots-\lambda_{n}\xi_{n}^{+}+\mu_{1}\eta_{1}^{-}+\cdots+\mu_{m}\eta_{m}^{-}\big).

With Proposition
5.7
we obtain for the action on the coherent state
\mathsf{K}_{\tau}
,

\displaystyle\big({:}\hat{F}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}}{:}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big)(\phi)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{2}}\{\mu_{1}\eta_{1}+\cdots+\mu_{m}\eta_{m},\phi\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{2}}\{\tau,\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\xi_{n}\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

With the relation (
5.14
) we obtain

\displaystyle\big({:}\hat{E}{:}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big)(\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{n+m}}\{\eta_{1},\phi\}_{\Sigma}\cdots\{\eta_{m},\phi\}_{\Sigma}\{\tau,\xi_{1}\}_{\Sigma}\cdots\{\tau,\xi_{n}\}_{\Sigma}\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\{\tau,\phi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\big(a_{\eta_{1}}^{\dagger}\cdots a_{\eta_{m}}^{\dagger}a_{\xi_{1}}\cdots a_{\xi_{n}}\mathsf{K}_{\tau}\big)(\phi).

In the last equality we have used the formulas (
3.8
) and (
3.11
) for the action of creation and annihilation operators.
∎

Finally, we consider the semiclassical interpretation of coherent states and correlation functions for slice observable. This can be seen as a limiting case of the case of general regions treated in Section
3.4.2
. Thus, we fix a normalized coherent state
\mathsf{k}_{\tau}
and consider the expectation value of the Weyl slice observable
F
in this state. With relation (
5.2
) and the Structure Theorem
3.1
we get

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathsf{k}_{\tau},\hat{F}\mathsf{k}_{\tau}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{F}\big(\mathsf{k}_{\tau}\otimes\mathsf{k}_{\tau}\big)=\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}(\mathsf{k}_{\tau}\otimes\mathsf{k}_{\tau})F(\hat{(\tau,\tau)})\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{F}(\mathsf{K}_{0}\otimes\mathsf{K}_{0})

\displaystyle=\langle\mathsf{k}_{\tau},\mathsf{k}_{\tau}\rangle_{\Sigma}F((\tau,\tau))\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)=F^{\prime}(\tau)\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

So, up to the vacuum expectation value, this is precisely the value of the classical slice observable
F
on the classical solution
\tau
. What is more, moving to normal ordered quantization the vacuum expectation value is removed, compare equation (
3.27
). Thus, we get for arbitrary observables
F

\big\langle\mathsf{k}_{\tau},{:}\hat{F}{:}\mathsf{k}_{\tau}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=F^{\prime}(\tau).

5.2
\boldsymbol{*}
-structure and GNS construction

It turns out that the same representation of the slice observables on a Hilbert space can be obtained in a rather different manner. Namely, we may start with the quantum algebra of slice observables and construct a Hilbert space representation via the GNS construction. To this end we need the
*
-structure on the algebra and a positive
*
-functional corresponding to the vacuum correlation function. We detail this in the present section. Fix a hypersurface
\Sigma
.

The vacuum correlation functions of slice observables in the sense of Section
2
can be viewed as defining a linear functional
v_{\Sigma}\colon\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{C}
on the algebra of slice observables,

v_{\Sigma}(F)\coloneqq\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

(5.15)

For Weyl slice observables we have evaluated this in Section
4.3
leading to equation (
4.10
). This is in the context where
D^{\prime}\colon L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
defines a linear slice observable determined by an element
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
via formula (
4.2
), compare Figure
9
.
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
denotes the induced Weyl slice observable. Thus, in our present notation,

v_{\Sigma}(F)=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

(5.16)

Note that the expression
\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}
is not necessarily positive or even real for general complex
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
. It is strictly positive, however, for non-vanishing real
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}
. The coincidence of the present formula with the results of Section
5.1
follows here from the fact that the relevant formula (
3.25
) of Theorem
3.1
is simply identical to formula (
2.6
) underlying equation (
5.16
).

It is easy to see that
v_{\Sigma}
is compatible with the
*
-structure of
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}
, i.e.,
v_{\Sigma}(F^{*})=\overline{v_{\Sigma}(F)}
. Similarly,
v_{\Sigma}
defines in fact a positive functional. We limit ourselves here to remark that the linear observable
D
is self-adjoint with respect to the
*
-structure,
D^{*}=D
, precisely if the corresponding element
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
is real, i.e.,
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}
.
20
20

20

We do not include an independent proof of the positivity of
v_{\Sigma}
here, because we know already from Section
5.1
that it is positive, since
v_{\Sigma}(F^{*}\star F)=\rho_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{F^{*}\bullet F}(\mathsf{K}_{0}\otimes\mathsf{K}_{0})=\langle\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{0},\hat{F}\mathsf{K}_{0}\rangle_{\Sigma}\geq 0
for arbitrary
F
.

Following the GNS construction we define a hermitian sesquilinear form
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\Sigma}
on
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}
via

[G,F]_{\Sigma}\coloneqq v_{\Sigma}(G^{*}\star F).

(5.17)

For Weyl slice observables
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
,
G=\exp(\mathrm{i}E)
determined by linear observables
D
and
E
which in turn are determined by elements
\xi_{D},\xi_{E}\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
via equation (
4.2
) as in Section
4.2
, we obtain

\displaystyle[G,F]_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=v_{\Sigma}\big(\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}\big({-}\overline{\xi_{E}},\xi_{D}\big)\big)G^{*}\cdot F\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}\big({-}\overline{\xi_{E}},\xi_{D}\big)\big)\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\big\{\xi_{D}-\overline{\xi_{E}},\xi_{D}-\overline{\xi_{E}}\big\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\xi_{D},\xi_{D}\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\big\{\overline{\xi_{E}},\overline{\xi_{E}}\big\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\big\{\xi_{D},\overline{\xi_{E}}\big\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

(5.18)

Note that
\{\phi,\eta\}=0
for any
\eta\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
iff
\phi\in L_{\Sigma}^{+}
.
From this we may deduce that the left ideal
\mathcal{I}_{\Sigma}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}
on which the sesquilinear form vanishes is generated by the relation
F\sim\mathbf{1}
for the Weyl observables
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
with
\xi_{D}\in L_{\Sigma}^{+}
. According to the GNS construction the Hilbert space on which
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}
will be represented, may now be obtained as the completion of the quotient
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{I}_{\Sigma}
. This Hilbert space turns out to be naturally isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
. Indeed, for real
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}
we may identify the normalized coherent state
\mathsf{k}_{\xi}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
with the (equivalence class of the) Weyl observable
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
, where the linear observable
D
is determined by
\xi
via equation (
4.2
). Doing so, we realize that (
5.18
) precisely recovers the (normalized version) of the inner product of coherent states (
3.13
). What is more, by comparing relation (
4.8
) with equation (
5.8
) one may appreciate that the induced action of
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
coincides precisely with the one given by Proposition
5.1
.

5.3
Boundary observables as states

Consider again the correlation function of a Weyl observable
F
in a region
M
with a normalized coherent state
\mathsf{k}_{\xi}
on the boundary, compare Section
3.3
and Figure
7
. As we have learned in Section
5.1
, the state
\mathsf{k}_{\xi}
can be obtained from the vacuum by acting with a Weyl slice observable. In the present case this slice observable will be associated with the slice region
\hat{\partial M}
determined by the boundary
\partial M
of
M
. We call slice observables with the slice region determined by a boundary also
boundary observables
. Here, let
\xi\in L_{\partial M}
define the linear boundary observable
E
via
E^{\prime}(\phi)=2\omega_{\partial M}(\xi,\phi)
and
G=\exp(\mathrm{i}E)
be the corresponding Weyl observable. Then, by equation (
5.8
), we have
\hat{G}\mathsf{K}_{0}=\mathsf{k}_{\xi}
. This implies

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{F}(\mathsf{k}_{\xi})

\displaystyle=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(\hat{G}\mathsf{K}_{0}\big)=\sum_{k\in I}\big\langle\zeta_{k},\hat{G}\mathsf{K}_{0}\big\rangle_{\partial M}\rho_{M}^{F}(\zeta_{k})=\sum_{k\in I}\rho_{\hat{\partial M}}^{G}(\mathsf{K}_{0}\otimes\iota_{\partial M}(\zeta_{k}))\rho_{M}^{F}(\zeta_{k})

\displaystyle=\rho_{M}^{G\bullet F}(\mathsf{K}_{0}).

Here, the third equality arises from equation (
5.2
) while the forth is the composition rule in the form (
3.29
), where
c=1
. In other words, the correlation function for a Weyl observable
F
on a normalized coherent state
\mathsf{k}_{\xi}
is the same as the correlation function of the product Weyl observable
G\bullet F
on the vacuum state. Compare Figure
7
to Figure
11
. With relation (
3.26
), we may write this as

\rho_{M}^{F}(\mathsf{k}_{\xi})=\rho_{M}^{G\bullet F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

(5.19)

Since
F
may be an arbitrary Weyl observable, this is valid for any observable
F
.

F
M
\partial M
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}K_{0}}
G=\exp(\mathrm{i}E)
\xi

Figure 11:
Spacetime region
M
containing an observable
F
with Weyl slice observable
G
determined by
\xi
on the boundary.

What formula (
5.19
) implies is that the role played by a normalized coherent state (here
\mathsf{k}_{\xi}
) can be completely captured through a corresponding boundary Weyl observable (here
G
). Moreover, since general states can be constructed from coherent states via derivatives, recall equation (
3.17
), the role played by any state can be captured through a suitable boundary observable in this way. Remarkably, the right-hand side of equation (
5.19
) does not make any use of a Hilbert space of states. In contrast to the left-hand side, it is perfectly well-defined even if the polarization on the boundary
\partial M
is not a Kähler polarization.
This suggests an approach to quantization where the role of states is played by slice observables and no Hilbert space of states need to be constructed. What is more, this notion of quantization would work for non-Kähler polarizations as well. This is the subject of the following section.

6
Quantization without Hilbert spaces

In this section we develop an approach to quantization based on slice observables instead of states and applicable to any polarization, as suggested in Section
5.3
.

6.1
Correlation functions with boundary observables

Consider a region
M
and a polarization
L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}\subseteq L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
transverse to the polarization
L_{\tilde{M}}^{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
, that is
L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}=L_{\tilde{M}}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
. For an element
\tau\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
we denote its unique decomposition by
\tau=\tau^{\rm int}+\tau^{\rm ext}
, where
\tau^{\rm int}\in L_{\tilde{M}}^{\mathbb{C}}
and
\tau^{\rm ext}\in L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
.
Let
F\colon K_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
denote an observable in the interior of
M
. Additionally, consider a slice observable
G\colon K_{\hat{\partial M}}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
on the boundary
\partial M
of
M
. In this context we introduce the notation

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}_{G}\big)\coloneqq\rho_{M}^{G\bullet F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big),

where the right-hand side is defined by equation (
2.6
).
This suggests that
W^{\rm P}_{G}
represents a “state” determined by the boundary observable
G
. For the moment, however, this is merely a notation, and we do not take
W^{\rm P}_{G}
(or similarly the “vacuum state”
W^{\rm P}
) itself to stand for any mathematical object.
21
21

21

One might of course simply think of
W^{\rm P}_{G}
as the pair
\big(L_{\partial M}^{\rm P},G\big)
, i.e., as an element of the Cartesian product
\mathcal{P}_{\partial M}\times\mathcal{A}_{\partial M}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{\partial M}
is the space of polarizations on
\partial M
, i.e., Lagrangian subspaces of
L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
.
However, since choosing a polarization and a slice observable also makes sense on an oriented hypersurface that is not necessarily the boundary of a region, we occasionally use the notation in this more general context.

Suppose
G
is a Weyl slice observable
G=\exp(\mathrm{i}\,E)
with the linear slice observable
E
determined by
\xi\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
according to equations (
4.1
) and (
4.2
). This is illustrated in Figure
11
(disregard
\mathsf{K}_{0}
there). We also use the more specific notation

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)\coloneqq\rho_{M}^{G\bullet F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

(6.1)

In terms of our symbolic notation we may write
K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\coloneqq W^{\rm P}_{G}
. Note also
K^{\rm P}_{0}=W^{\rm P}
. This of course suggests a coherent state. Indeed, this is motivated by the identity (
5.19
), valid in the case of a Kähler polarization and when
\xi\in L_{\partial M}
is real. That is,

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}(\mathsf{k}_{\xi}).

(6.2)

The coherent state here is normalized. If on the other hand (still in the Kähler case) we replace
\xi
with the projection
P^{-}(\xi)
we obtain instead the ordinary (non-normalized) coherent state, compare Corollary
5.3
,

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{P^{-}(\xi)}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}(\mathsf{K}_{\xi}).

(6.3)

Since this is valid for arbitrary observables
F
and even arbitrary regions
M
, we write equations (
6.2
) and (
6.3
) informally as
K^{\rm P}_{\xi}=\mathsf{k}_{\xi}
and
K^{\rm P}_{P^{-}(\xi)}=\mathsf{K}_{\xi}
, and extend them thus to hypersurfaces that are not necessarily boundaries of regions. Note, however, that this makes sense only with a fixed choice of hypersurface orientation and Kähler polarization.

Finally, formula (
3.16
) for the wave function of an
n
-particle state in Kähler quantization suggests the following definition. Let
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Define the boundary observable
G
by

G^{\prime}(\phi)=\prod_{k=1}^{n}4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\partial M}(\xi_{k},\phi).

(6.4)

Then set

Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}\coloneqq W^{\rm P}_{G}.

(6.5)

In the case of a Kähler polarization we may take
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}\in L_{\partial M}
and note
4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\partial M}(P^{-}(\xi_{k}),\phi)=\{\xi_{k},\phi\}_{\partial M}
so that by Proposition
5.4
we obtain (in our informal notation)

Q^{\rm P}_{P^{-}(\xi_{1}),\dots,P^{-}(\xi_{n})}=\psi,

(6.6)

where
\psi
is the
n
-particle state with wave function (
3.16
). We also note the general relation

Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}=\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{n}2\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}\bigg)K^{\rm P}_{\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\xi_{n}}\bigg|_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}=0}.

(6.7)

If we replace
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}
by
P^{-}(\xi_{1}),\ldots,P^{-}(\xi_{n})
in the Kähler case we recover expression (
3.17
).

We briefly consider momentum states in the standard example of Klein–Gordon theory on an equal-time hypersurface in Minkowski space. Comparing (
6.6
) with (
3.22
) we have

Q^{\rm P}_{P^{-}(\phi_{p_{1}}),\ldots,P^{-}(\phi_{p_{n}})}=\sqrt{2^{n}}\psi_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}.

(6.8)

Since Weyl observables generate all other observables, and similarly coherent states generate all other states we focus primarily on “states” of the form
K^{\rm P}_{\xi}
, i.e., Weyl boundary observables.
If
F
is also a Weyl observable, we can evaluate
\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)
explicitly by using formula (
2.6
). Thus, let
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}\,D)
with
D\colon K_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
linear. Let
\phi\in A_{M}^{D+E,\mathbb{C}}\cap L^{\rm P}_{\partial M}
be the unique solution of the modified equations of motion satisfying the boundary condition on
\partial M
. To obtain a more explicit form of the amplitude expression we need to know
\phi
in different pieces of spacetime. In the interior of
M
we write
\phi_{M}
while in the exterior we write
\phi_{X}
, see Figure
12
.

F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
M
\phi_{M}\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}
\phi_{X}\in L_{\partial M}^{P}
\partial M
G=\exp(\mathrm{i}E)
\xi

Figure 12:
Spacetime region
M
containing a Weyl observable
F
with Weyl slice observable
G
determined by
\xi
on the boundary. The interior solution is marked
\phi_{M}
and the exterior one
\phi_{X}
.

From (
4.3
) we get,
\phi_{X}=\phi_{M}-\xi
. On the other hand we require
\phi_{X}\in L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
and
\phi_{M}\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}
. To satisfy the latter requirement we can set
\phi_{M}=\eta+\Delta
, where
\Delta\in L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}
, while
\eta\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}\cap L^{\rm P}_{\partial M}
unique. We get
\phi_{X}=\eta+\Delta-\xi
.
\Delta
is thus determined by the requirement
\Delta-\xi\in L^{\rm P}_{\partial M}
. Decomposing
\xi=\xi^{\rm int}+\xi^{\rm ext}
we get
\Delta=\xi^{\rm int}
.
We obtain

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)

\displaystyle=\rho_{M}^{G\bullet F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(D+E)(\phi)\bigg)=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\phi_{M})+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}E^{\prime}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}(\phi_{M}+\phi_{X})\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta+\xi^{\rm int})+\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\bigg(\xi,\eta+\xi^{\rm int}-\frac{1}{2}\xi\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D\big(\xi^{\rm int}\big)+\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\big(\xi,\eta+\xi^{\rm int}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D\big(\xi^{\rm int}\big)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D\big(\xi^{\rm int}\big)+\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\big(\xi,\xi^{\rm int}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta)\bigg)F\big(\xi^{\rm int}\big)\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\big(\xi,\xi^{\rm int}\big)\big).

(6.9)

We can formulate the result analogous to the Factorization Theorem
3.1
as follows.

Theorem 6.1
.

If
F
is the Weyl observable
\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
, then,

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)=\rho_{M}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)F\big(\xi^{\rm int}\big)\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big),\quad\text{with}

(6.10)

\displaystyle\rho_{M}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)=\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\left(\xi,\xi^{\rm int}\right)\right),

(6.11)

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta)\bigg).

(6.12)

Here
\eta\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}\cap L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
.

We already know that in the Kähler case and for
\xi\in L_{\partial M}
we must recover Theorem
3.1
. Nevertheless, it is instructive to see this explicitly. Thus, we have to bring into exact correspondence the three factors on the right-hand side of (
3.23
) with the three factors on the right-hand side of (
6.10
). We have already commented extensively on the equality of the third factor, see equations (
5.19
) and (
6.1
). To see the equality for the two remaining factors decompose
\xi=\xi^{\rm R}+J_{\partial M}\xi^{\rm I}
with
\xi^{\rm R},\xi^{\rm I}\in L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}
as in Section
3.3
. Then, it is easily verified that
\xi^{\rm int}=\xi^{\rm R}-\mathrm{i}\xi^{\rm I}=\hat{\xi}
. In particular,
F\big(\xi^{\rm int}\big)=F\big(\hat{\xi}\big)
, which shows equality for the second factor. For the remaining factor we have

\displaystyle\rho_{M}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\big(\xi,\xi^{\rm int}\big)\big)=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\big(J_{\partial M}\xi^{\rm I},\xi^{\rm int}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,g_{\partial M}\big(\xi^{\rm I},\xi^{\rm int}\big)\bigg)=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,g_{\partial M}\big(\xi^{\rm I},\xi^{\rm R}-\mathrm{i}\xi^{\rm I}\big)\bigg)=\rho_{M}(\mathsf{k}_{\xi}).

As expected, we confirm the equality (
6.2
) for any Weyl observable
F
and thus for any observable
F
(since the Weyl observables generate all observables).

Back to the general polarization setting, for later use we consider the special case that the slice observable
G
(and thus also
E
) satisfies the following
interior translation invariance
condition:
G^{\prime}(\phi+\delta)=G^{\prime}(\phi)
for any
\phi\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
and any
\delta\in L_{\tilde{M}}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Here this is equivalent to
\xi\in L_{\tilde{M}}^{\mathbb{C}}
and thus
\xi=\xi^{{\rm int}}
. We can then rewrite (
6.9
) as

\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta)\bigg)F\big(\xi^{\rm int}\big)=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta)\bigg)\exp(2\mathrm{i}\,\omega(\xi,\eta))=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta)\bigg)G^{\prime}(\eta).

Linearity in
G
of the right-hand expression shows that this generalizes to arbitrary slice observables
G
that satisfy the interior translation invariance condition.

Lemma 6.2
.

If
F
is the Weyl observable
\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
,
\eta\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}\cap L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
, and
G
is an interior translation invariant slice observable, then

\rho_{M}^{G\bullet F}\big(W^{{\rm P}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)\,G^{\prime}(\eta).

(6.13)

6.2
Normal ordering and semiclassical interpretation

We recall the generalized notion of normal ordering of the Kähler setting, given by the defining relation (
3.27
) in Section
3.1
. There is no difficulty in further generalizing this to the present setting with general polarizations. Thus, for
F\colon K_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
an observable in
M
and
\xi\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
we define

\rho_{M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(K_{\xi}^{\rm P}\big)\coloneqq\rho_{M}\big(K_{\xi}^{\rm P}\big)F\big(\xi^{\rm int}\big).

(6.14)

In the Kähler case take
\xi\in L_{\partial M}
and note
(P^{-}(\xi))^{\rm int}=\xi^{\rm int}=\hat{\xi}
to recover (
3.27
).

For the special case of
F
being a Weyl observable we obtain by comparison of definition (
6.14
) with the factorization identity (
6.10
) the simple relation

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K_{\xi}^{\rm P}\big)=\rho_{M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(K_{\xi}^{\rm P}\big)\,\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

By linearity this relation extends to arbitrary states, (i.e., arbitrary boundary observables). To emphasize this we chose a notation
X^{\rm P}
for the state, which really stands for
W^{\rm P}_{G}
with
G
an arbitrary boundary observable,

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(X^{\rm P}\big)=\rho_{M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(X^{\rm P}\big)\,\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

(6.15)

In analogy to the coherent states in the Kähler quantization setting we can ask for a semiclassical interpretation of the Weyl slice observables represented by the notation
K^{\rm P}_{\xi}
, compare Section
3.4
. As for the semiclassical interpretation of amplitudes (compare Section
3.4.1
), we do not have here an analogue of the tunneling interpretation afforded by formula (
3.24
). Instead, with the corresponding formula (
6.11
) we can merely affirm that the amplitude is unity if
\xi
is an interior solution, i.e., if
\xi=\xi^{\rm int}
. If this is not the case, i.e., we consider a solution “not allowed” in the interior, there need not in general be a suppression. Since in the absence of an inner product we also do not have a notion of normalization of the “state”
K^{\rm P}_{\xi}
this need not be a cause for concern.

As for the semiclassical interpretation of correlation functions for
K^{\rm P}_{\xi}
, the considerations of the Kähler case (Section
3.4.2
) largely still hold. In particular for
\xi=\xi^{\rm int}
an interior solution, we obtain for Weyl observables the classical expectation value
F(\xi)
, see formula (
6.10
). Moreover, for normal ordered quantization this extends to arbitrary observables, compare formula (
6.14
). This is quite a strong indication that
K^{\rm P}_{\xi}
(or something like
K^{\rm P}_{P^{-}(\xi)}
, see above) provides a good semiclassical description of the classical solution
\xi
near the boundary
\partial M
, up to normalization.

6.3
Changing the vacuum

It is a natural question to consider what happens when the vacuum, i.e., the polarization determining the vacuum changes. More precisely, consider a region
M
and
two polarizations
L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
and
L_{\partial M}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
, both transverse to
L_{\tilde{M}}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Is there then a “state”
Y^{{\rm P}}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
with respect to the unprimed vacuum that mimics the primed vacuum, i.e., such that all correlation functions coincide? That is, we want

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(Y^{{\rm P}}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}\big)

for all observables
F
in
M
. As usual, it is sufficient to consider this for the special case of Weyl observables
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
with
D
linear.

Let us write

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}\big)=q\cdot\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{{\rm P}}\big).

(6.16)

Inserting the explicit formula (
2.6
) for the vacuum correlation functions yields

q=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)\bigg).

Here,
\eta\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}\cap L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
and
\eta^{\prime}\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}\cap L_{\partial M}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
. Note that
\eta^{\prime}-\eta\in L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}
, so we can use equation (
2.5
) to get

q=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}(\eta^{\prime}-\eta,\eta)\big)=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}(\eta^{\prime},\eta)\big).

With the obvious notation we notice,
\eta=\eta^{\rm ext}
and
\eta^{\prime}=(\eta^{\prime})^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}}=(\eta+(\eta^{\prime}-\eta))^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}}=\eta^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}}
. Define
G^{\prime}\colon L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
by

G^{\prime}(\phi)=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\big(\phi^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},\phi^{{\rm ext}}\big)\big).

(6.17)

Then,
q=G^{\prime}(\eta)
. Consider the slice observable
G
determined by
G^{\prime}
. As is easily seen,
G
is interior translation invariant and Lemma
6.2
applies. Comparison of the right-hand side of equation (
6.16
) with equation (
6.13
) thus yields

Y^{{\rm P}}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}=W^{{\rm P}}_{G}.

Proposition 6.3
.

For any observable
F
in
M
we have

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}_{G}\big).

By allowing the observable
F
itself to be a product of a slice observable and a general observable we obtain an apparent generalization.

Corollary 6.4
.

For any slice observable
H
in
\partial M
and any observable
F
in
M
we have

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}_{H}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}_{G\star H}\big).

6.4
Composition via observables

In the setting of Hilbert spaces on hypersurfaces constructed from Kähler polarizations, the composition of correlation functions for regions can be accomplished via a sum over a complete basis of the Hilbert space associated to the gluing hypersurface. This was reviewed in Section
3.5
. Equivalently (and often more conveniently), the sum is replaced by a complete integral over coherent states. However, the present emphasis on slice observables over Hilbert space states suggests using the former also to accomplish composition. This turns out to be not only possible, but natural, induced by thinking of the composition as effected by a change of polarization. What is more, no gluing anomaly occurs for this manner of composing.

Figure 13:
Composition via slice observables: Setup for composition (left) with boundary polarization
L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
, region composed along slice observable
\hat{\Sigma}
(right) with boundary polarization
L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}
, and location of boundary observable
G
within region.

Thus, let
M
be a region with boundary
\partial M=\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}
, where
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a copy of
\Sigma
. Let
M_{1}
denote the gluing of
M
to itself along
\Sigma
with
\Sigma^{\prime}
. We can think of the gluing as effected by the formation of a slice region
\hat{\Sigma}
with boundary
\partial\hat{\Sigma}=\overline{\Sigma}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}
, see Figure
13
.
We consider a polarization
L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}\subseteq L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
transversal to
L_{\tilde{M}}^{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Similarly, we consider a polarization
L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}\subseteq L_{\partial M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}
transversal to
L_{\tilde{M}_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq L_{\partial M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}
. It is then clear that the Lagrangian subspace
L_{\partial M}^{({\rm P}_{1},\text{m})}\coloneqq\big(L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}\oplus L_{\overline{\hat{\Sigma}}}^{\mathbb{C}}\big)\subseteq L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
intersects
L_{\tilde{M}}^{\mathbb{C}}
only in
0
, but we require full transversality,
L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}=L_{\partial M}^{({\rm P}_{1},\text{m})}\oplus L_{\tilde{M}}^{\mathbb{C}}
. In the following we write the decomposition
L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}=L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}\oplus L_{\tilde{M}}^{\mathbb{C}}
for elements as
\phi=\phi^{\rm ext}+\phi^{\rm int}
. Similarly, we write the decomposition
L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}=L_{\partial M}^{({\rm P}_{1},\text{m})}\oplus L_{\tilde{M}}^{\mathbb{C}}
for elements as
\phi=\phi^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}}+\phi^{{\rm int}^{\prime}}
.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
D\colon K_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
be a linear observable in
M
and
D_{1}\colon K_{M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
the induced linear observable in
M_{1}
. Set
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}\,D)
and
F_{1}=\exp(\mathrm{i}\,D_{1})
. Then,

\rho_{M_{1}}^{F_{1}}\big(W^{{\rm P}_{1}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(Y^{{\rm P}}_{({\rm P}_{1},\text{m})}\big).

(6.18)

Proof.

Define
G^{\prime}\colon L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
as

G^{\prime}(\phi)=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial_{M}}\big(\phi^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},\phi^{{\rm ext}}\big)\big).

This defines a slice observable
G
in
M
and a state
Y^{{\rm P}}_{({\rm P}_{1},\text{m})}=W^{\rm P}_{G}
that encodes a change of polarization from
{\rm P}
to
({\rm P}_{1},\text{m})
. Now let
\eta^{\prime}\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}\cap L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1},\text{m}}
. Then, we have

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(Y^{{\rm P}}_{({\rm P}_{1},\text{m})}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{({\rm P}_{1},\text{m})}\big)=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta^{\prime})\bigg).

On the other hand let
\eta_{1}\in A_{M_{1}}^{D_{1},\mathbb{C}}\cap L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}
. By Axiom (C7) of
[
40
, Section 4.6]
we have
D_{1}(\eta_{1})=D(\eta^{\prime})
. But,

\rho_{M_{1}}^{F_{1}}\big(W^{{\rm P}_{1}}\big)=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D_{1}(\eta_{1})\bigg).

Thus, we obtain relation (
6.18
).
∎

One aspect of this composition theorem that is not quite satisfactory is the fact that the slice observable
G
acts not only on
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
and
L_{\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}}^{\mathbb{C}}
, but also on
L_{\partial M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}
, where no gluing takes place. Of course with polarizations before and after composition completely independent, this cannot be otherwise. However, in case that the polarization is unchanged on
\partial M_{1}
, one might hope for the composition to be effectable by a slice observable
G
not dependent on
L_{\partial M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}
. This hope is justified as we show in the following. Thus, suppose that
L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}=L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}\oplus L_{\overline{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}}^{{\rm P}_{\Sigma}}
.

For
\phi\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}=L_{\partial M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus L_{\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}}^{\mathbb{C}}
we use the notation
\phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})
. With this we define a slice observable
G
on
\partial M
via

G^{\prime}(\phi)=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},(0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}}\big)\big).

(6.19)

Note that this slice observable is not interior translation invariant. However, it has two other invariance properties.

Lemma 6.6
.

G
as defined above has the following properties:

(1)

G^{\prime}(\phi+(\delta_{1},0,0))=G^{\prime}(\phi)
for any
\phi\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
and any
