\delta_{1}\in L_{\partial M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

(2)

G^{\prime}\big(\phi+\big(0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\big)^{\rm int}\big)=G^{\prime}(\phi)
for any
\phi\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
and any
\big(\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\big)\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus L_{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

The first property follows immediately since the definition (
6.19
) makes no reference to the first component
\phi_{1}
of the argument
\phi
. As for the second property, it suffices to show this for the argument of the exponential in (
6.19
). We evaluate the left-hand side of the property (2) to be demonstrated order by order in
\delta
. That is, we have to show that both the first and the second order in
\delta
vanish. We introduce the notation
(\cdot)_{\rm x00}
to mean projection onto the first component. Similarly,
(\cdot)_{\rm 0xx}
means projection onto the second and third components. We start with the first order expression in
\delta
:

\displaystyle\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},(((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx})^{{\rm ext}}\big)+\omega_{\partial M}\big((((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx})^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},(0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ {}=\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})-(0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}^{\prime}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx}-(((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx})^{{\rm int}}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ \phantom{=}{}-\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})-(0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx}-(((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx})^{{\rm int}^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ {}=\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}}),(0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int}\big)-\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}}),(((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx})^{{\rm int}}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ \phantom{=}{}-\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}^{\prime}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx}\big)-\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}}),(0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ \phantom{=}{}+\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}}),(((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx})^{{\rm int}^{\prime}}\big)+\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ {}=-\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx}\big)-\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}^{\prime}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ \phantom{=}{}+\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx}\big)+\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ {}=2\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx}\big)-2\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}^{\prime}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ {}=2\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}}-(0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}^{\prime}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm 0xx}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ {}=2\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}}-(0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}^{\prime}},(0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int}-((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm x00}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ {}=-2\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}}-(0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm int}^{\prime}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm x00}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ {}=2\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}}-(0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int})_{\rm x00}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ {}=2\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}}-(0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},(-(0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm ext})_{\rm x00}\big)=0.

The last equality follows, because both arguments of the symplectic form have first components in the Lagrangian subspace
L_{M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}
.
It remains to show that the term of second order in
\delta
also vanishes. This is

\omega_{\partial M}\big(\big(\big((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int}\big)_{\rm 0xx}\big)^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},\big(\big(\big(0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\big)^{\rm int}\big)_{\rm 0xx}\big)^{{\rm ext}}\big).

However, if we set
(0,\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})\coloneqq\big((0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int}\big)_{\rm 0xx}
this reduces to the previous demonstration. This completes the proof.
∎

It turns out that this gives us an analogue of Lemma
6.2
.

Lemma 6.7
.

If
F
is the Weyl observable
\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
,
\eta\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}\cap L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
, and
G
is a slice observable with the stated invariance properties, then

\rho_{M}^{G\bullet F}\big(W^{{\rm P}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)\,G^{\prime}(\eta).

(6.20)

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
6.2
, we may at first assume that
G
is a Weyl observable determined by
G^{\prime}(\phi)=\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}(\xi,\phi)\right)
with
\xi\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
. By the first invariance property
\xi_{1}=0
and by the second invariance property
\omega_{\Sigma}\left(\xi,(0,\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{\rm int}\right)=0
for any
\big(\delta_{\Sigma},\delta_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\big)\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus L_{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{C}}
. But this implies
\omega_{\partial M}(\xi,\xi^{\rm int})=0
and thus equation (
6.20
) as in the proof of Lemma
6.2
.
∎

With this we are ready to show that the slice observable
G
given by expression (
6.19
) effects the desired composition.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
D\colon K_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
be a linear observable in
M
and
D_{1}\colon K_{M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
the induced linear observable in
M_{1}
. Set
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}\,D)
and
F_{1}=\exp(\mathrm{i}\,D_{1})
. Then,

\rho_{M_{1}}^{F_{1}}\big(W^{{\rm P}_{1}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{{\rm P}}_{G}\big).

(6.21)

Proof.

Let
\eta\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}\cap\Big(L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}\oplus L_{\overline{\partial\hat{\Sigma}}}^{{\rm P}_{\Sigma}}\Big)
. By Lemma
6.7
we have

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{{\rm P}}_{G}\big)=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta)\bigg)G^{\prime}(\eta).

Now let
\eta^{\prime}\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}\cap L_{\partial M}^{({\rm P}_{1},\text{m})}
. Then,

\displaystyle G^{\prime}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\eta_{\Sigma},\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},(0,\eta_{\Sigma},\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\big((0,\eta_{\Sigma},\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}}+(\eta_{1},0,0)^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},(0,\eta_{\Sigma},\eta_{\Sigma^{\prime}})^{{\rm ext}}+(\eta_{1},0,0)^{{\rm ext}}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\big(\eta^{{\rm ext}^{\prime}},\eta^{{\rm ext}}\big)\big)=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}\big(\eta^{\prime},\eta\big)\big).

With this we get

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{{\rm P}}_{G}\big)=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta)\bigg)\exp(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial M}(\eta^{\prime},\eta))=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D(\eta^{\prime})\bigg).

On the other hand let
\eta_{1}\in A_{M_{1}}^{D_{1},\mathbb{C}}\cap L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}
. By Axiom (C7) of
[
40
, Section 4.6]
we have
D_{1}(\eta_{1})=D(\eta^{\prime})
. But,

\rho_{M_{1}}^{F_{1}}\big(W^{{\rm P}_{1}}\big)=\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}D_{1}(\eta_{1})\bigg).

Thus, we obtain relation (
6.21
).
∎

6.5
Wick’s theorem

In the remainder of this Section
6
we explore how various basic tools of textbook QFT generalize to the present framework. We start with Wick’s theorem. That is, we consider correlation functions of monomial observables as in Section
2.2
, but for general states rather than the vacuum state. To this end, we resort, as usual, to normal ordering, here as defined in Section
6.2
. In particular, we recall equation (
6.15
) for Weyl observables. Its right hand side serves as the generating function of Wick’s theorem. Thus, for the product
D_{1}\cdots D_{n}
we find on the arbitrary state
X^{\rm P}

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{D_{1}\cdots D_{n}}\big(X^{\rm P}\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\bigg({-}\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}\bigg)\bigg)\rho_{M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(X^{\rm P}\big)\,\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)\bigg|_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{\sigma\in S^{n}}\frac{1}{(2m)!\,(n-2m)!}\bigg(\bigg(\prod_{k=2m+1}^{n}\bigg({-}\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{\sigma(k)}}\bigg)\bigg)\rho_{M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(X^{\rm P}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\times\bigg(\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{2m}\bigg({-}\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{\sigma(k)}}\bigg)\bigg)\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)\bigg)\bigg|_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{\sigma\in S^{n}}\frac{1}{(2m)!\,(n-2m)!}\rho_{M}^{{:}D_{\sigma(2m+1)}\cdots D_{\sigma(n)}{:}}\big(X^{\rm P}\big)\,\rho_{M}^{D_{\sigma(1)}\cdots D_{\sigma(2m)}}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{\sigma\in S^{n}}\frac{1}{2^{m}\,m!\,(n-2m)!}\rho_{M}^{{:}D_{\sigma(2m+1)}\cdots D_{\sigma(n)}{:}}\big(X^{\rm P}\big)\,\prod_{j=1}^{m}\,\rho_{M}^{D_{\sigma(2j-1)}D_{\sigma(2j)}}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

Here
\lfloor a\rfloor
denotes the “floor” of
a
, i.e., the largest integer smaller or equal to
a
.

We remark the particular cases of a linear observable
D
and a quadratic observable
E
,

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{D}\big(X^{\rm P}\big)

\displaystyle=\rho_{M}^{{:}D{:}}\big(X^{\rm P}\big),

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{E}\big(X^{\rm P}\big)

\displaystyle=\rho_{M}^{{:}E{:}}\big(X^{\rm P}\big)+\rho_{M}^{E}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

6.6
Connected amplitude and multi-particle states

We proceed to have a closer look at multi-particle states. We first consider the free amplitude for a multi-particle state. Let
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, recalling (
6.7
) and (
6.11
),

\displaystyle\rho_{M}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{n}2\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}\bigg)\rho_{M}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\xi_{n}}\big)\bigg|_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}=0}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{n}2\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\mathrm{i}\sum_{k,l=0}^{n}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{l}\omega_{\partial M}\big(\xi_{k},\xi_{l}^{\rm int}\big)\bigg)\bigg|_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}=0}.

This vanishes if
n
is odd. Otherwise, set
n=2m
and we get

\displaystyle\rho_{M}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{2m}2\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}\bigg)\frac{1}{m!}\bigg(\mathrm{i}\sum_{k,l=0}^{n}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{l}\omega_{\partial M}\big(\xi_{k},\xi_{l}^{\rm int}\big)\bigg)^{m}\bigg|_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\sigma\in S^{2m}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\partial M}\big(\xi_{\sigma(2j-1)},\xi_{\sigma(2j)}^{\rm int}\big).

This can be interpreted in the usual way, namely that there is no interaction and particles are merely pairwise identified.
We proceed to consider the same amplitude with a Weyl observable
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
present. This may serve as a generating function for the corresponding amplitude of an interacting theory. Thus, with Theorem
6.1
,

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{n}2\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}\bigg)\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\xi_{n}}\big)\bigg|_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}=0}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\!2\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}\bigg)\rho_{M}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\xi_{n}}\big)F\big(\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}^{\rm int}\!+\cdots\!+\lambda_{n}\xi_{n}^{\rm int}\big)\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)\bigg|_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{\sigma\in S^{n}}\frac{1}{(2m)!\,(n-2m)!}\bigg(\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{2m}2\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{\sigma(k)}}\bigg)\rho_{M}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\xi_{n}}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\times\bigg(\bigg(\prod_{k=2m+1}^{n}2\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{\sigma(k)}}\bigg)F\big(\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}^{\rm int}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\xi_{n}^{\rm int}\big)\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)\bigg)\bigg|_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{\sigma\in S^{n}}\frac{1}{(2m)!(n\!-\!2m)!}\rho_{M}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{\sigma(1)},\dots,\xi_{\sigma(2m)}}\big)\bigg(\prod_{k=2m+1}^{n}\!\!\!\!2\mathrm{i}D\big(\xi^{\rm int}_{\sigma(k)}\big)\bigg)\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

The parameter
m
in the outermost sum can be interpreted as follows. The contribution with given value
m
corresponds to
m
pairs of particles being identified and not interacting, while the other
n-2m
particles participate in the interaction. In terms of diagrams this may be expressed as
m
lines that connect
2m
particles into pairs while the remaining particles are connected to
F
. This motivates the definition of the
connected
amplitude for coherent states as

\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)\coloneqq\frac{\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)}{\rho_{M}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)}=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi^{\rm int}}\big).

We observe that the denominator never vanishes. The second equality is obtained by explicit inspection, using the results of Theorem
6.1
. Note that by linearity this definition and the equality are valid for arbitrary (not necessarily Weyl) observables
F
and thus for
F
possibly defining an interacting theory.

For multi-particle states this yields

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}\big)=\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{\sigma\in S^{n}}\frac{1}{(2m)!\,(n-2m)!}\,\rho_{M}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{\sigma(1)},\dots,\xi_{\sigma(2m)}}\big)\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{\sigma(2m+1)},\dots,\xi_{\sigma(n)}}\big),

as well as its “inverse”,

\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}\big)=\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{\sigma\in S^{n}}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{(2m)!\,(n-2m)!}\,\rho_{M}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{\sigma(1)},\dots,\xi_{\sigma(2m)}}\big)\rho_{M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{\sigma(2m+1)},\dots,\xi_{\sigma(n)}}\big).

Moreover, we have

\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1}^{\rm int},\dots,\xi_{n}^{\rm int}}\big).

(6.22)

Again, these expressions are valid for
F
an arbitrary observable.

It is straightforward to carry out the same steps for
normal ordered quantization
. In particular, we can make the analogous definition of connected amplitudes. In that case an additional simplification occurs, and we get

\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(K^{{\rm P}}_{\xi}\big)=\rho_{M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(K^{{\rm P}}_{\xi^{\rm int}}\big)=F\big(\xi^{\rm int}\big).

For multi-particle states this translates to

\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1}^{\rm int},\dots,\xi_{n}^{\rm int}}\big)=\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{n}2\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}\bigg)F\big(\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}^{\rm int}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\xi_{n}^{\rm int}\big)\bigg|_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}=0}.

In the case of a Weyl observable
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
this specializes to

\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1}^{\rm int},\dots,\xi_{n}^{\rm int}}\big)=\prod_{k=1}^{n}2\mathrm{i}D\big(\xi^{\rm int}_{k}\big).

On the other hand, for Weyl observables
F
on general states we have the analogue of equation (
6.15
),

\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{F}\big(X^{{\rm P}}\big)=\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{{:}F{:}}\big(X^{{\rm P}}\big)\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

6.7
LSZ reduction

The LSZ reduction procedure in QFT allows re-expressing transition amplitudes between initial and final particle states in terms of vacuum
n
-point functions, compare Section
1.6
. As previously mentioned, this provides one motivation for the proposal of the present section where we actually define “states” in terms of observables in the vacuum. This indeed turns out to make the LSZ reduction formula (
1.11
) essentially tautological. Nevertheless, we have to correctly identify the observables involved.

We begin by recalling from Section
6.1
the way multi-particle states are represented in our setting. Let
M
be a region with exterior polarization
L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
transversal to
L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}\allowbreak\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
E_{k}
define the linear boundary observable defined by
E_{k}^{\prime}(\xi)=4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\partial M}(\xi_{k},\phi)
. Let
G
be the product boundary observable (
6.4
), i.e.,
G=E_{1}\cdots E_{n}
. By definition, we have (
6.5
). That is, with an interior observable
F
representing an interaction we have

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{E_{1}\cdots E_{n}\bullet F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

In the reduction formula (
1.11
) we care about the connected amplitude, however, signaled by separating off on the right-hand side the “disconnected terms”. In our setting we obtain the corresponding result by using equation (
6.22
). To this end we define the boundary observables
\tilde{E}^{\prime}_{k}(\phi)=4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\partial M}\big(\xi_{k}^{\rm int},\phi\big)
. Then,

\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1}^{\rm int},\dots,\xi_{n}^{\rm int}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{\tilde{E}_{1}\cdots\tilde{E}_{n}\bullet F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

(6.23)

We claim that this is precisely the generalization of the LSZ reduction formula (
1.11
) to our setting.

In the remainder of this section we show how formula (
1.11
) arises as a special case of formula (
6.23
). As a first step, we take a closer look at the linear slice observables
\tilde{E}_{k}
. Suppose for the moment that
F
is a Weyl observable
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
. Take
\eta
to be a complexified solution of the equations of motion modified by
D
, i.e.,
\eta\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}
. Then,

\tilde{E}^{\prime}_{k}(\eta)=4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\partial M}\big(\xi_{k}^{\rm int},\eta\big)=2\mathrm{i}D\big(\xi_{k}^{\rm int}\big),

where the second equality arises from relation (
2.5
). We assume further that the equations of motion are given in the form

(\mathsf{D}\phi)(x)=0,

where
\mathsf{D}
is a differential operator in spacetime. We let
D
be given by a source
j\colon M\to\mathbb{C}
,

D(\phi)=\int_{M}\mathrm{d}x\,\phi(x)j(x).

Then,
\eta\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}
means that
\eta
is a solution of the inhomogeneous equations of motion with source
j
. In particular,

(\mathsf{D}\eta)(x)=j(x).

With this we can write

\tilde{E}^{\prime}_{k}(\eta)=2\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\mathrm{d}x\,\xi_{k}^{\rm int}(x)(\mathsf{D}\eta)(x).

(6.24)

Note that this equation makes no longer reference to the source
j
or the observable
D
. So this is valid for
\eta\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}
for all possible
D
. We conclude that it is valid for arbitrary configurations
\eta\in K_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}
.
22
22

22

One might object that the configuration space
K_{M}
might include configurations too irregular (e.g., non-differentiable) for this to make sense. However, we (intentionally) have not provided a formal definition of
K_{M}
anywhere. It turns out that a good definition of
K_{M}
appears to be precisely to take it to be generated by the spaces
A_{M}^{D}
for all
D
. We will not provide any evidence for this claim in the present paper, however.
This also means that we can drop the requirement for
F
to be a Weyl observable.

In order to recover expression (
1.11
) we now specialize to Klein–Gordon theory in Minkowski spacetime. Moreover, we take the region
M
to be determined by a time interval,
M=[t_{1},t_{2}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which we extend to infinity,
t_{1}\to-\infty
,
t_{2}\to\infty
. Thus, consider a momentum state on the boundary
\partial M=\Sigma_{t_{1}}\sqcup\overline{\Sigma}_{t_{2}}
with incoming momenta
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}
and outgoing momenta
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
. Set

\xi_{k}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}P^{-}((\phi_{q_{k}},0)),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau_{l}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}P^{-}((0,\phi_{p_{l}})).

Then,
Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n},\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m}}
describes the state in question, compare (
6.8
). With this, the left-hand side of (
1.11
) translates to our notation as

\langle p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}|q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}\rangle=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n},\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m}}\big),

where, again,
F
encodes the interactions. As for the “disconnected terms” in (
1.11
),

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n},\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m}}\big)=\text{disconnected terms}+\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n},\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m}}\big).

It remains to rewrite the connected amplitude in terms of boundary observables. To this end define boundary observables by
B^{\prime}_{k}(\phi)\coloneqq 4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\partial M}\big(\xi_{k}^{\rm int},\phi\big)
and
C^{\prime}_{l}(\phi)\coloneqq 4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\partial M}\big(\tau_{l}^{\rm int},\phi\big)
. Thus, by the generalized LSZ formula (
6.23
) we have

\rho_{{\rm c},M}^{F}\big(Q^{\rm P}_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n},\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m}}\big)=\rho_{M}^{B_{1}\cdots B_{n}\cdot C_{1}\cdots C_{m}\bullet F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)=\langle 0|\mathbf{T}B_{1}\cdots B_{n}C_{1}\cdots C_{m}|0\rangle.

(6.25)

Here we have used on the right-hand side standard textbook notation. To understand the observables
B_{k}
and
C_{l}
we note that
(P^{-}(\xi))^{\rm int}=\xi^{\rm int}
for arbitrary
\xi\in L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Also, as is easily checked,

(\phi_{q_{k}},0)^{\rm int}(t,x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{\rm e}^{-\mathrm{i}(E_{q_{k}}t-q_{k}x)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(0,\phi_{p_{l}})^{\rm int}(t,x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}(E_{p_{l}}t-p_{l}x)}.

Combining this with equation (
6.24
) we get for the boundary observables

B^{\prime}_{k}(\phi)=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}^{3}x\,{\rm e}^{-\mathrm{i}(E_{q_{k}}t-q_{k}x)}(\mathsf{D}\phi)(x),\qquad C^{\prime}_{l}(\phi)=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}^{3}x\,{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}(E_{p_{l}}t-p_{l}x)}(\mathsf{D}\phi)(x).

Inserting this into (
6.25
) yields by linearity

\displaystyle\rho_{M}^{B_{1}\cdots B_{n}\cdot C_{1}\cdots C_{m}\bullet F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)=\mathrm{i}^{n+m}\!\int\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{n}\mathrm{d}^{4}y_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}^{4}y_{m}\exp\bigg(\mathrm{i}\sum_{l=1}^{m}p_{l}\cdot y_{l}-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=1}^{n}q_{k}\cdot x_{k}\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\rho_M^{B_1\cdots B_n\cdot C_1\cdots C_m\bullet F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)=}{}\times\mathsf{D}_{x_{1}}\cdots\mathsf{D}_{x_{n}}\mathsf{D}_{y_{1}}\cdots\mathsf{D}_{y_{m}}\langle 0|\mathbf{T}\phi(x_{1})\cdots\phi(x_{n})\phi(y_{1})\cdots\phi(y_{m})|0\rangle.

Here, 4-dimensional notation is used that translates as,
q_{k}\cdot x=E_{q_{k}}t-q_{k}x
and
p_{l}\cdot y=E_{p_{l}}t-p_{l}y
. Thus, we recover also the right-hand side of the LSZ formula (
1.11
).

7
Inner products and Hilbert spaces of states

7.1
General polarizations, real structure and inner product

We recall that a
real structure
on a complex vector space
V
is a complex conjugate linear involution
\alpha\colon V\to V
. If
V
arises as the complexification
V=L^{\mathbb{C}}

of a real vector space
L
, then complex conjugation given by

a+\mathrm{i}b\mapsto a-\mathrm{i}b
for
a,b\in L
defines a real structure.
Conversely, given a real structure on
V
, define
V^{\alpha}
as the real subspace invariant under
\alpha
. Then
V
arises as the complexification of
V^{\alpha}
with complex conjugation given by
\alpha
. Suppose that
V
is equipped additionally with a complex bilinear symplectic form
\omega\colon V\times V\to\mathbb{C}
. We say that the real structure
\alpha
is
compatible
with the symplectic form
\omega
if for all
x,y\in V

\omega(\alpha(x),\alpha(y))=\overline{\omega(x,y)}.

(7.1)

In that case,
\omega
arises as the complexification of a real valued symplectic form on
V^{\alpha}
.

Let
(V,\omega)
be a complex symplectic vector space with compatible real structure
\alpha
. Define the sesquilinear form
V\times V\to\mathbb{C}
given for
\phi,\eta\in V
by

(\phi,\eta)^{\alpha}\coloneqq 4\mathrm{i}\omega\left(\alpha(\phi),\eta\right).

(7.2)

As is easy to verify, this sesquilinear form is non-degenerate and hermitian. If
V=L^{\mathbb{C}}
and
\alpha
is given by complex conjugation this is just the sesquilinear form (
1.6
). Consider a complex Lagrangian subspace
V^{\rm P}\subseteq V
on which this sesquilinear form is positive-definite. If
V=L^{\mathbb{C}}
and
\alpha
is complex conjugation, this defines precisely a Kähler polarization. In the general case, we call this an
\alpha
-Kähler polarization
. The conjugate
V^{\overline{\rm P}}\coloneqq\alpha(V^{\rm P})
of
V^{\rm P}
with respect to
\alpha
is a Lagrangian subspace of
V
transversal to
V^{\rm P}
.

Consider a complex symplectic vector space
(V,\omega)
with a pair of transversal Lagrangian subspaces,
V^{\rm P}
and
V^{\overline{\rm P}}
. That is, we have
V=V^{\rm P}\oplus V^{\overline{\rm P}}
. We write
x=x^{\rm P}+x^{\overline{\rm P}}
for the corresponding decomposition of elements of
V
. Let
J\colon V\to V
be the associated complex structure. That is,
J
is the complex linear map equal to
\mathrm{i}\id
on
V^{\rm P}
and equal to
-\mathrm{i}\id
on
V^{\overline{\rm P}}
. Note that we automatically have compatibility with the symplectic form,
\omega(Jx,Jy)=\omega(x,y)
. We may then define a complex bilinear form, compare equation (
3.2
),

\{x,y\}\coloneqq 2\omega(x,Jy)+2\mathrm{i}\omega(x,y)=4\mathrm{i}\omega\big(x^{\overline{\rm P}},y^{\rm P}\big).

(7.3)

This form is sesquilinear with respect to
J
, but it is clearly degenerate.

Suppose the complex symplectic vector space
(V,\omega)
is equipped with a compatible real structure
\alpha
and an
\alpha
-Kähler polarization
V^{\rm P}\subseteq V
. Note that
J\circ\alpha=\alpha\circ J
. Restricting the bilinear form (
7.3
) to the real subspace
V^{\alpha}
makes it hermitian and positive-definite. What is more, the positive-definite inner products (
7.2
) on
V^{\rm P}
and (
7.3
) are related for
\phi,\eta\in V^{\alpha}
as

\big(\phi^{\rm P},\eta^{\rm P}\big)^{\alpha}=\{\phi,\eta\}.

(7.4)

In the case where
V=L^{\mathbb{C}}
is the complexification of
L
and
\alpha
is given by complex conjugation we recover precisely the usual Kähler polarization setting and the relation between the positive-definite inner products on
L^{+}=L^{\rm P}
and on
L=(L^{\mathbb{C}})^{\alpha}
given by (
7.4
) becomes (
3.5
). This is then the basis for the usual Kähler quantization of the phase space
L
.

In general the situation of interest is the following: We are given a real phase space
L
with symplectic form
\omega
. Moreover, we are given a pair
L^{\rm P}
,
L^{\overline{\rm P}}
of transversal complex Lagrangian subspaces of
L^{\mathbb{C}}
, (encoding vacua on the two sides of a hypersurface). The task is then to find a suitable compatible real structure
\alpha
on
L^{\mathbb{C}}
so that
L^{\rm P}
encodes an
\alpha
-Kähler polarization. That is,
(i)

\alpha
has to be a compatible real structure,
(ii)

\alpha
has to interchange
L^{\rm P}
and
L^{\overline{\rm P}}
, and
(iii)
, the bilinear form (
7.2
) has to be positive-definite on
L^{\rm P}
.

7.2
Modified
\boldsymbol{*}
-structure and GNS construction

We suppose in this section that we are given on the complexified symplectic phases space
(L_{\Sigma},\omega_{\Sigma})
a compatible real structure
\alpha_{\Sigma}\colon L_{\Sigma}\to L_{\Sigma}
as well as an
\alpha
-Kähler polarization
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}\subseteq L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
. We then show that this induces a modified
*
-structure on the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}
of slice observables. Moreover, the GNS construction, carried out in analogy to Section
5.2
, but with respect to the modified
*
-algebra, yields a Hilbert space of states.

If the real structure on
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
is changed, then this affects the
*
-structure on the classical algebra of slice observables in a canonical way. Namely, the standard structure (
5.7
) is replaced by,

F^{\alpha}(\phi)\coloneqq\overline{F\left(\alpha(\phi)\right)}.

(7.5)

Note that we write
F^{\alpha}
instead of
F^{*}
to distinguish this
*
-structure from the standard one. This
*
-structure also carries over to the quantum algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}
because the defining relation (
4.8
) of the latter is compatible with it. This holds true for the present
*
-structure in the same way as it does for the usual complex conjugation, since the relation (
4.8
) does not refer to any specific compatible real structure. We denote by
\mathcal{A}^{\alpha}_{\Sigma}
the quantum algebra of slice observables with
*
-structure induced from the compatible real structure
\alpha_{\Sigma}
.

We recall the linear functional
v_{\Sigma}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\alpha}_{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{C}
encoding the vacuum and given by the vacuum correlation function (
5.15
). Its evaluation on a Weyl observable
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
was discussed in Section
4.3
. The result is given by equation (
4.9
), where the linear observable
D
is determined by
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
via relation (
4.2
). In terms of the bilinear form (
7.3
) this is

v_{\Sigma}(F)=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg),

directly generalizing the notation of the Kähler case, see equation (
4.10
). If
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
is not a Kähler polarization this is not a positive functional on
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}
, but since
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
is an
\alpha
-Kähler polarization it is a positive functional on
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
.

The sesquilinear form on
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
induced by
v_{\Sigma}
is now given by

[G,F]_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\coloneqq v_{\Sigma}(G^{\alpha}\star F),

generalizing expression (
5.17
). Moreover, for Weyl slice observables
F
and
G
determined by
\xi_{D},\xi_{E}\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
as in Section
5.2
, we get, generalizing (
5.18
),

[G,F]_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\xi_{D},\xi_{D}\}_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\{\alpha_{\Sigma}(\xi_{E}),\alpha_{\Sigma}(\xi_{E})\}_{\Sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\{\xi_{D},\alpha_{\Sigma}(\xi_{E})\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

In complete analogy to the Kähler case (Section
5.2
) the left ideal
\mathcal{I}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
on which the sesquilinear form vanishes is generated by the relation
F\sim\mathbf{1}
for the Weyl observables
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
with
\xi_{D}\in L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
. The Hilbert space of the GNS construction on which
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
will be represented is then obtained as the completion of the quotient
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}/\mathcal{I}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
. We denote this Hilbert space by
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
.

As in the Kähler case, the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
may alternatively be constructed as a Fock space or as a space of square-integrable
J_{\Sigma}
-holomorphic functions on
L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
. All its properties (including particle states and coherent states) are exactly as outlined in Section
3.1
, except that we have to replace
L_{\Sigma}
everywhere by
L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
and complex conjugation on
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}=(L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha})^{\mathbb{C}}
by the map
\alpha_{\Sigma}
. What is more, the whole quantization scheme outlined in Section
3.1
including hypersurface orientation reversal and hypersurface decomposition carries over to the
\alpha
-Kähler setting. Note that this requires
\alpha_{\Sigma}
to be the same for both hypersurface orientations. That is,
\alpha_{\overline{\Sigma}}=\alpha_{\Sigma}
, which we assume from now onwards. It also raises the issue of locality for
\alpha_{\Sigma}
. That is, for a hypersurface decomposition,
\alpha_{\Sigma}
needs to decompose accordingly, compare the similar discussion for Kähler polarizations at the end of Section
3.1
. With a consistent assignment of compatible real structures
\alpha_{\Sigma}
to hypersurfaces
\Sigma
(in addition to polarizations) we again satisfy Axioms (T1), (T1b), (T2), (T2b) of Appendix
B
upon quantization. What is more, since the functional
v_{\Sigma}
is determined by the correlation function (
5.15
), the GNS construction automatically ensures that Axiom (T3x) of Appendix
B
is satisfied, as in the Kähler case.

7.3
Correlation functions and composition

We proceed to explore the interplay between the quantization based on observables presented in Section
6
and the Hilbert spaces constructed in this section, at the level of amplitudes and correlation functions. Firstly, it is clear that correlation functions continue to be well-defined by Theorem
6.1
. We merely have to reinterpret the correlation functions in terms of boundary observables as correlation functions in terms of boundary states, as prescribed by equations (
6.2
), (
6.3
) and (
6.6
). One might then expect to precisely recover Theorem
3.1
describing the structure of correlation functions in the Kähler polarization case, as long as we replace everywhere
L_{\partial M}
by
L_{\partial M}^{\alpha}
. There is a subtlety, however. Namely, there is an implicit assumption of compatibility of complex conjugation with the complexified interior space of solutions
L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
. More precisely, the assumption is that
L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}
is invariant under complex conjugation. Since
L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}
is the complexification of
L_{M}
, this is trivially the case. However, when we generalize to the
\alpha
-Kähler polarization, this condition takes the form

\alpha_{\partial M}\big(L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\big)=L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}.

(7.6)

If this holds we may replace everywhere
L_{M}
by
L_{M}^{\alpha}\coloneqq\big\{\phi\in L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\colon\alpha_{\partial M}(\phi)=\phi\big\}
. Except for the substitutions, Theorem
3.1
then holds as stated. Otherwise, even the formulation of Theorem
3.1
as given in Section
3.3
does not necessarily make sense. This is because it relies on the real orthogonal decomposition (with respect to
g_{\partial M}^{\alpha}
),

L_{\partial M}^{\alpha}=L_{M}^{\alpha}\oplus J_{\partial M}L_{M}^{\alpha}.

In the Kähler case this is
[
35
, Lemma 4.1]
. We shall refer to the compatibility condition (
7.6
) as
interior compatibility
.

Let us emphasize again that even without interior compatibility, the correlation functions are perfectly well-defined on the states of
\mathcal{H}_{\partial M}^{\alpha}
with their structure described by Theorem
6.1
. To more explicitly relate the boundary observables and states induced by their action, we note that by the
\alpha
-Kähler version of Corollary
5.5
we have for
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}

K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\cong\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi,\tau)\right)\mathsf{k}^{\alpha}_{\tau},

(7.7)

where
\tau\coloneqq P^{-}_{\Sigma}(\xi)+\alpha_{\Sigma}(P^{-}_{\Sigma}(\xi))
.
This informal notation really stands for an equality of correlation functions,

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)=\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\omega_{\partial M}(\xi,\tau)\right)\rho_{M}^{F}(\mathsf{k}^{\alpha}_{\tau}).

Here,
F
is an arbitrary observable. We write a superscript
\alpha
for the coherent states in
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}_{\Sigma}
. Recalling the discussion of Section
6.2
, relation (
7.7
) also clarifies the semiclassical interpretation of the coherent states in the
\alpha
-Kähler setting. That is, even though the latter are not labeled by real solutions they can be given a semiclassical interpretation through the mapping

\xi\mapsto P^{-}_{\Sigma}(\xi)+\alpha_{\Sigma}(P^{-}_{\Sigma}(\xi)),

when
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}
is real.

As for the axiomatic system of GBQFT of Appendix
B
with respect to amplitudes, we note that we satisfy Axiom (TO4). Also, there is no difficulty in satisfying the disjoint composition Axiom (TO5a). For the self-composition Axiom (TO5b), however, we might expect that the corresponding Theorem
3.3
in the Kähler case does depend on the interior compatibility condition (
7.6
). There is certainly no difficulty if this is satisfied for all involved regions. Note that in the special case of a slice region, interior compatibility is satisfied as long as
\alpha
is the same on both boundary hypersurfaces. This implies in particular that Proposition
5.1
on the action of slice observables on the Hilbert space of states carries over straightforwardly to the
\alpha
-Kähler setting, providing agreement with the GNS construction as in the Kähler case.

It turns out that even when the interior compatibility condition is not satisfied, a suitably generalized version of the composition Theorem
3.3
holds, satisfying Axiom (TO5b). To recall the context (see Section
3.3
for the Kähler case), consider a region
M
with boundary
\partial M=\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}
, where
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a copy of
\Sigma
. Denote by
M_{1}
the region obtained by gluing
M
to itself along
\Sigma
with
\Sigma^{\prime}
, see Figure
8
. We have polarizations
L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}\subseteq L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
and
L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}\subseteq L_{\partial M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}
, transversal to the interior polarizations
L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
and
L_{M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq L_{\partial M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}
respectively.

We shall make the additional assumption that an element in
L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}
“extends” to an element in
L_{\partial M}^{{\rm P}}
exactly in the same way as a solution in
L_{M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}
extends to a solution in
L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}
by Axiom (C7), equation (
A.1
) of Appendix
A
. This is justified by thinking of elements in
L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}
,
L_{\partial M}^{{\rm P}}
as “exterior solutions”, compare
[
8
]
. Note that this assumption is stronger than in Theorem
6.5
, where the polarizations
L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
and
L_{\partial M_{1}}^{{\rm P}_{1}}
are completely independent. On the other hand it is weaker than in Theorems
3.3
and
6.8
, where
L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
factorizes in terms of the decomposition of
\partial M
.

We shall not need to assume
\alpha
-Kähler polarizations. We shall merely assume that we are given a real structure
\alpha_{\Sigma}\colon L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
on
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
(not necessarily compatible with
\omega_{\Sigma}
). Moreover, we assume we are given a real positive definite inner product
g_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\colon L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\times L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\to\mathbb{R}
making
L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
into a real separable Hilbert space. We do not assume that this is related to the symplectic form or a complex structure as e.g., in equation (
3.3
). Recall that the inner product gives rise to a Gaussian measure
\nu_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
, or simply denoted
\nu
, on an extension
\hat{L}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
of the vector space
L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}

[
35
]
, compare also Section
3.1
. We formulate the composition rule in the following not in terms of a sum over a complete basis (as in Axiom (TO5b) of Appendix
B
or as in Section
3.3
), but equivalently in terms of an integral over
\hat{L}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
, using a completeness relation analogous to formula (
3.14
). For relevant discussion of this point, see
[
35
,
40
]
. What is more, we formulate the gluing rule only for Weyl observables, analogous to coherent states. As usual this is sufficient.

Definition 7.1
.

We say the gluing is
admissible
if the function

\xi\mapsto\rho_{M}\big(K^{\rm P}_{(0,\xi,\xi)}\big)\,\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}g_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}(\xi,\xi)\bigg)

is
integrable
, i.e., is an element of
\mathrm{L}^{1}\big(\hat{L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}}\big)
. In this case the
gluing anomaly factor
is defined as

c\big(M;\Sigma,\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}\big)\coloneqq\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}}\rho_{M}\big(K^{\rm P}_{(0,\xi,\xi)}\big)\,\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}g_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}(\xi,\xi)\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(\xi).

(7.8)

Theorem 7.2
.

In the above context, suppose the gluing is admissible. Let
D\colon K_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
be a linear observable in
M
and
D_{1}\colon K_{M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
the induced linear observable in
M_{1}
. Set
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}\,D)
and
F_{1}=\exp(\mathrm{i}\,D_{1})
. Then, for any
\phi\in L_{\partial M_{1}}^{\mathbb{C}}
,

\rho_{M_{1}}^{F_{1}}\big(K^{{\rm P}_{1}}_{\phi}\big)\,c\big(M;\Sigma,\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}\big)=\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}}\rho_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{(\phi,\xi,\xi)}\big)\,\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}g_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}(\xi,\xi)\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(\xi).

The proof is provided in Appendix
C
.

7.4
Wave function of vacuum change

If there is a Hilbert space of states as in the traditional Kähler quantization scheme (Section
3
) or the present generalized
\alpha
-Kähler quantization scheme, it is natural to ask what state encodes a change of vacuum. The corresponding question for slice observables was answered in Section
6.3
. Thus, to get this state we would apparently just have to let this observable, determined by equation (
6.17
), act on the vacuum state
\mathsf{K}_{0}
of the Hilbert space in the sense of Section
5.1
or in the generalized sense of the
\alpha
-Kähler setting of the present section. Since the observable is the exponential of a quadratic form this is not quite as straightforward as considering a Weyl observable for example. One difficulty arises from the fact that we cannot in general expect a vacuum to live as a state in the Hilbert space build on a different vacuum. This manifests itself in that the “state” we are looking for is not normalizable in general. However, it turns out that it is well-defined as a (generally non-square-integrable) holomorphic wave function. We shall call it a
pseudo-state
.

The basis for our considerations will be the relation between vacuum states and amplitudes
[
8
]
. Consider a hypersurface
\Sigma
and choose transversal polarizations
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
and
L_{\Sigma}^{\overline{\rm P}}
. We take
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
to encode the vacuum on the side
\Sigma
of the hypersurface while
L_{\Sigma}^{\overline{\rm P}}
encodes it on the opposite side, given by
\overline{\Sigma}
. Suppose we have a compatible positive-definite real structure
\alpha
and have performed the corresponding
\alpha
-Kähler quantization yielding a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
. We now consider a polarization
L_{\Sigma}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}\subseteq L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
on
\Sigma
, different from
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
, but such that it is still transversal to
L_{\Sigma}^{\overline{\rm P}}
.
Denote the pseudo-state encoding the polarization
L_{\Sigma}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
on
\Sigma
by
Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
, in analogy to the notation in Section
6.3
for the corresponding slice observable. We now imagine that
\overline{\Sigma}
is the boundary
\partial X
of a region
X
whose space
L_{X}^{\mathbb{C}}
of interior solutions is precisely given by the polarization
L_{\partial X}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
on
\overline{\Sigma}
, i.e.,
L_{X}^{\mathbb{C}}\coloneqq L_{\partial X}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
.
23
23

23

We recall that although the notation seems to suggest it, it is not meant to imply that
L_{X}^{\mathbb{C}}
is the complexification of some real subspace
L_{X}
.
For any state
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{\overline{\Sigma}}^{\alpha}
we then have
24
24

24

Strictly speaking the inner product might not exist for all states
\psi
. It does exist, however, for coherent states
\psi
, which is sufficient.

\rho_{X}(\psi)=\big\langle\iota_{\overline{\Sigma}}(Y^{{\rm P}}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}),\psi\big\rangle_{\overline{\Sigma}}=\big\langle\iota_{\Sigma}(\psi),Y^{{\rm P}}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}.

This relation follows from the duality between amplitudes and vacuum wave functions
[
8
]
. Alternatively, we can also see this as an implication of the composition rule (Theorem
7.2
together with its disjoint version) if we imagine
\psi
replaced by a region on the other side of
X
with an arbitrary observable in it.

To obtain the wave function of
Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
we use the reproducing property (
3.12
) of coherent states,

Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}(\xi)=\big\langle\mathsf{K}^{\alpha}_{\xi},Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=\big\langle\iota_{\Sigma}\big(\mathsf{K}^{\alpha}_{\xi}\big),Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=\rho_{X}\big(\mathsf{K}_{\xi}^{\alpha}\big).

Note that we use the same notation
\mathsf{K}^{\alpha}_{\xi}
for the corresponding coherent state on either side (i.e., orientation) of the hypersurface
\Sigma
. We proceed to evaluate the amplitude
\rho_{X}
with equation (
6.11
) of Theorem
6.1
, taking into account relation (
6.3
). Denote the decomposition
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}=L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}\oplus L_{\Sigma}^{{\overline{\rm P}}}
by
\xi=\xi^{+}+\xi^{-}
and
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}=L_{\Sigma}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}\oplus L_{\Sigma}^{{\overline{\rm P}}}
by
\xi=\xi^{\mathrm{X}}+\xi^{\mathrm{M}}
. Then, for
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\rho_{X}\big(\mathsf{K}_{\xi}^{\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle=\rho_{X}\big(K^{\overline{\rm P}}_{\xi^{+}}\big)=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial X}\big(\xi^{+},(\xi^{+})^{X}\big)\big)=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\partial X}\big(\xi^{+},\xi^{X}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\xi^{X},\xi^{+}\big)\big).

We note that the right-hand side does not explicitly depend on the region
X
. That is, it makes sense for any hypersurface
\Sigma
, depending only on the polarizations
L_{\Sigma}^{{\rm P}}
,
L_{\Sigma}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
and
L_{\Sigma}^{{\overline{\rm P}}}
, with the condition that each of the first two is transversal to the third. We take this to mean that the pseudo-state
Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
and its wave function make sense on hypersurfaces that are not necessarily boundaries of a region. We obtain the wave function

Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}(\xi)=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\xi^{X},\xi^{+}\big)\big)=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{4}\big\{\xi^{X},\xi\big\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

(7.9)

This formula is even independent of the choice of
\alpha_{\Sigma}
. However, given a compatible choice of
\alpha_{\Sigma}
, it is easily seen to define a holomorphic function on
L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
with respect to the complex structure
J_{\Sigma}
.
By construction, the wave function (
7.9
) is normalized to yield unity when taking its inner product with the standard vacuum. Whether it can be normalized to correspond to an ordinary state in the Hilbert space depends on the polarizations. The standard criterion in the case of Kähler polarizations is that the difference between the corresponding complex structures has to be a Hilbert–Schmidt operator
[
49
]
.

To facilitate the interpretation of formula (
7.9
) in terms of a superposition of multi-particle states we use the completeness relation (
3.15
) for
L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
. With this we can rewrite the wave function (
7.9
) as

\displaystyle Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}(\xi)=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{8}\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}}\big\{\xi^{X},\phi\big\}_{\Sigma}\{\phi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\phi)\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}'}(\xi)}{}=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{8}\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}}\big\{\tilde{\phi},\xi\big\}_{\Sigma}\{\phi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\phi)\bigg).

(7.10)

Here,
\tilde{\phi}\coloneqq(\phi^{+})^{M}+\alpha\big((\phi^{+})^{M}\big)
.
The significance of the integrand of the right-hand expression is that we can interpret it plainly as a 2-particle wave function with quantum numbers
\phi
and
\tilde{\phi}
respectively, compare expression (
3.16
). It is also instructive to rewrite this in terms of creation operators, compare expression (
3.8
),

Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\int_{\hat{L}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}}a^{\dagger}_{\tilde{\phi}}\,a^{\dagger}_{\phi}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Sigma}(\phi)\bigg)\mathsf{K}^{\alpha}_{0}.

7.5
Vacuum change and Bogoliubov coefficients

The traditional approach to quantization in curved spacetime relies on choices of basis
[
3
]
. Accordingly, a change of vacuum is parametrized in terms of
Bogoliubov coefficients
that relate the relevant basis. We examine in the following how the traditional language and results are recovered from the present setting.
At the same time we generalize these results to the
\alpha
-Kähler setting.

We suppose the same setting as in the previous Section
7.4
. That is, we have a hypersurface
\Sigma
and transversal polarizations
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
and
L_{\Sigma}^{\overline{\rm P}}
. We also assume that we have a compatible positive-definite real structure
\alpha_{\Sigma}
. Let
\{u_{k}\}_{k\in I}
be an orthonormal basis of
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
with respect to the inner product (
7.2
). Then,
\{\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{k})\}_{k\in I}
is an orthonormal basis of
L_{\Sigma}^{\overline{\rm P}}
with respect to the negative of the inner product. We have

(u_{k},u_{l})_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}=\delta_{k,l},\qquad(\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{k}),\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{l}))_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}=-\delta_{k,l},\qquad(u_{k},\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{l}))_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}=0.

This is in complete analogy to the Kähler case (
3.1
), which we recover when
\alpha_{\Sigma}
is ordinary complex conjugation. Also, as in the Kähler case (
3.6
), the orthonormal basis
\{u_{k}\}_{k\in I}
is in one-to-one correspondence to an orthonormal basis
\{w_{k}\}_{k\in I}
of
L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
with respect to (
7.3
) via
w_{k}=u_{k}+\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{k})
.
We suppose that we have another polarization
L_{\Sigma}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
on
\Sigma
. We require that the inner product (
7.2
) is also positive-definite on
L_{\Sigma}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
. This implies that
L_{\Sigma}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
is transversal to
L_{\Sigma}^{{\overline{\rm P}}}
, an assumption made in Section
7.4
. Let
\{u_{k}^{\prime}\}_{\in I}
be an orthonormal basis of
L_{\Sigma}^{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
.

We may use the basis
\{w_{k}\}_{k\in I}
to expand the wave function of
Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
similar to the continuous expansion (
7.10
),

Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}(\xi)=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i\in I}\big\{\xi^{X},w_{i}\big\}_{\Sigma}\{w_{i},\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg)=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i\in I}\big\{u_{i}^{M},\xi\big\}_{\Sigma}\{w_{i},\xi\}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

Consider an
n
-particle state
\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
parametrized in terms of the same basis via

\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(\xi)\coloneqq\{w_{i_{1}},\xi\}_{\Sigma}\cdots\{w_{i_{n}},\xi\}_{\Sigma}.

In our conventions (compare Section
3.1
) the inner product between such states is given by

\langle\psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{n}},\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\rangle_{\Sigma}=2^{n}\sum_{\sigma\in S^{n}}\delta_{{j_{1}},i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\delta_{{j_{n}},i_{\sigma(n)}}.

We now consider the inner product between such a generic
n
-particle state and
Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}}
. If
n
is odd this vanishes, so suppose
n=2m
. As is easy to see we get

\big\langle Y^{\rm P}_{{\rm P}^{\prime}},\psi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{2m}}\big\rangle_{\Sigma}=\frac{(-1)^{m}}{m!}\sum_{\sigma\in S^{2m}}\Lambda_{i_{\sigma(1)},i_{\sigma(2)}}\cdots\Lambda_{i_{\sigma(2m-1)},i_{\sigma(2m)}}.

Here we have defined

\Lambda_{k,l}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in I}\big\{w_{k},\alpha\big(u_{i}^{M}\big)\big\}_{\Sigma}\{w_{l},w_{i}\}_{\Sigma}.

However, using orthogonality and further properties of the objects involved we get

\displaystyle\Lambda_{k,l}

\displaystyle=\big\{\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{k}),\alpha_{\Sigma}\big(u_{l}^{M}\big)\big\}_{\Sigma}=4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{k}),\alpha_{\Sigma}\big(u_{l}^{M}\big)\big)=4\mathrm{i}\overline{\omega_{\Sigma}\big(u_{k},u_{l}^{M}\big)}=4\mathrm{i}\overline{\omega_{\Sigma}\big(u_{k}^{X},u_{l}\big)}

\displaystyle=4\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\alpha_{\Sigma}\big(u_{k}^{X}\big),\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{l})\big)=\big(u_{k}^{X},\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{l})\big)^{\alpha}_{\Sigma}.

We expand
u_{k}^{X}
in terms of the basis
\{u^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
via

u_{k}^{X}=\sum_{j}c_{k,j}u_{j}^{\prime}.

The coefficients
c_{k,j}
can be related to the Bogoliubov coefficients in the expansion

u_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in I}a_{i,j}u_{j}+\sum_{j}b_{i,j}\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{j}),

where
a_{i,j}=(u_{j},u_{i}^{\prime})^{\alpha}_{\Sigma}
and
b_{i,j}=-(\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{j}),u_{i}^{\prime})^{\alpha}_{\Sigma}
.
To this end, consider

\delta_{i,k}=(u_{i},u_{k})^{\alpha}_{\Sigma}=\big(u_{i},u_{k}^{X}\big)^{\alpha}_{\Sigma}=\sum_{j\in I}c_{k,j}(u_{i},u_{j}^{\prime})^{\alpha}_{\Sigma}=\sum_{j\in I}c_{k,j}a_{j,i}.

That is,
c
as a matrix is inverse to
a
,
c_{k,j}=\big(a^{-1}\big)_{k,j}
. With this we may rewrite
\Lambda_{k,l}
as follows,

\Lambda_{k,j}=\sum_{j\in I}\overline{c_{k,j}}(u_{j}^{\prime},\alpha_{\Sigma}(u_{l}))^{\alpha}_{\Sigma}=-\sum_{j\in I}\overline{(a^{-1})_{k,j}b_{j,l}}.

Up to minor changes of conventions, this recovers in the Kähler case the well known formulas of DeWitt
[
11
]
.

7.6
Return to reality

Working with the space
L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
instead of with
L_{\Sigma}
might appear as an additional complication. This motivates looking for an identification of the two spaces in order to “pull back” all the structures from
L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
to
L_{\Sigma}
. This would allow expressing everything again in terms of
L_{\Sigma}
, as if we were working with a Kähler polarization. We shall assume here that we are given such an identification, i.e., a linear real bijection
I_{\Sigma}\colon L_{\Sigma}\to L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
.
Define

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}_{\Sigma}\colon\qquad L_{\Sigma}\times L_{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\text{by}\quad\tilde{\omega}_{\Sigma}(\phi,\eta)\coloneqq\omega_{\Sigma}(I_{\Sigma}(\phi),I_{\Sigma}(\eta)),

(7.11)

\displaystyle\tilde{J}_{\Sigma}\colon\qquad L_{\Sigma}\to L_{\Sigma},\qquad\quad\text{by}\quad\tilde{J}\phi\coloneqq I_{\Sigma}^{-1}(J_{\Sigma}I_{\Sigma}(\phi)),

(7.12)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{\Sigma}\colon\qquad L_{\Sigma}\times L_{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\,\text{by}\quad\tilde{g}_{\Sigma}(\phi,\eta)\coloneqq 2\tilde{\omega}_{\Sigma}(\phi,\tilde{J}_{\Sigma}\eta)=g_{\Sigma}(I_{\Sigma}(\phi),I_{\Sigma}(\eta)),

(7.13)

\displaystyle\{\cdot,\cdot\}^{\tilde{}}_{\Sigma}\colon\ \ L_{\Sigma}\times L_{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{C},\quad\text{by}\quad\{\phi,\eta\}^{\tilde{}}_{\Sigma}\coloneqq\tilde{g}_{\Sigma}(\phi,\eta)+2\mathrm{i}\tilde{\omega}_{\Sigma}(\phi,\eta)=\{I_{\Sigma}(\phi),I_{\Sigma}(\eta)\}_{\Sigma}.

(7.14)

Then,
\tilde{\omega}_{\Sigma}
is a symplectic form on
L_{\Sigma}
,
\tilde{J}_{\Sigma}
is a compatible complex structure,
\tilde{g}_{\Sigma}
is a real inner product and
\{\cdot,\cdot\}^{\tilde{}}_{\Sigma}
a complex inner product with respect to
\tilde{J}_{\Sigma}
. (We shall assume completeness for the inner products.) In the following we shall use the same notation
I_{\Sigma}
to denote the complexification of the map, i.e., its complex linear extension
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Pulling back the polarization with
I_{\Sigma}
leads to an ordinary Kähler polarization
L_{\Sigma}^{\tilde{{\rm P}}}\coloneqq I_{\Sigma}^{-1}\big(L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}\big)
.

With these ingredients we may construct a Hilbert space
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\Sigma}
based on
L_{\Sigma}
, and the modified structures
\tilde{\omega}_{\Sigma}
, etc., exactly in the usual way as outlined in Section
3.1
. This Hilbert space is then equivalent to
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
with the unitary equivalence given by the map
\tilde{U}_{\Sigma}\colon\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\Sigma}\to\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
which on coherent states takes the simple form

\tilde{U}_{\Sigma}\big(\tilde{\mathsf{K}}_{\xi}\big)=\mathsf{K}_{I_{\Sigma}(\xi)}^{\alpha}.

Here we denote the coherent states in
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\Sigma}
with a tilde and those in
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
with a superscript
\alpha
.
This equivalence extends to the creation and annihilation operators and their actions in the obvious way. It is also straightforward to express
\tilde{U}_{\Sigma}
in terms of holomorphic wave functions, mapping those on
L_{\Sigma}
(holomorphic with respect to
\tilde{J}_{\Sigma}
) to those on
L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
(holomorphic with respect to
J_{\Sigma}
). For
\tilde{\psi}\in\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\Sigma}
and
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}
we have

\tilde{U}_{\Sigma}\big(\tilde{\psi}\big)(I_{\Sigma}(\xi))=\tilde{\psi}(\xi).

We proceed to consider quantization in regions, i.e., the construction of amplitudes. Specifically, we are looking to reproduce an analogue of relation (
3.24
) of Theorem
3.1
, expressing the amplitude for coherent states in a region
M
in terms of the inner product
\tilde{g}_{\partial M}
. As already discussed in Section
7.3
to work at the level of the spaces
L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
this requires the interior compatibility condition for
\alpha_{\Sigma}
, (
7.6
). To additionally work at the level of the pull-back considered here, we also need
I_{\partial M}
to be compatible with the inclusion
L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq L_{\partial M}^{\mathbb{C}}
. That is, we need the
interior compatibility condition
for
I_{\partial M}
,

I_{\partial M}\big(L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}\big)=L_{M}^{\mathbb{C}}.

The induced amplitudes are, for
\tilde{\psi}\in\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\partial M}
,

\tilde{\rho}_{M}\big(\tilde{\psi}\big)=\rho_{M}\big(\tilde{U}_{\partial M}\big(\tilde{\psi}\big)\big).

With this, we have the analogue of relation (
3.24
), for
\xi\in L_{\partial M}
,

\tilde{\rho}_{M}\big(\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\xi}\big)=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\tilde{g}_{\partial M}\big(\xi^{\tilde{\mathrm{R}}},\xi^{\tilde{\mathrm{I}}}\big)-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{g}_{\partial M}\big(\xi^{\tilde{\mathrm{I}}},\xi^{\tilde{\mathrm{I}}}\big)\bigg).

(7.15)

Here
\xi=\xi^{\tilde{\mathrm{R}}}+\tilde{J}_{\Sigma}\xi^{\tilde{\mathrm{I}}}
with
\xi^{\tilde{\mathrm{R}}},\xi^{\tilde{\mathrm{I}}}\in L_{M}
. Equation (
7.15
) arises from (
3.24
) because we have
I_{\partial M}(\xi)^{\textrm{R}}=I_{\partial M}\big(\xi^{\tilde{\textrm{R}}}\big)
and
I_{\partial M}(\xi)^{\textrm{I}}=I_{\partial M}\big(\xi^{\tilde{\textrm{I}}}\big)
. In particular, equation (
7.15
) recovers precisely equation (
3.24
), but with the original structures replaced by the pulled back ones.
In essence, we obtain an equivalence between the original quantum theory with
\alpha
-Kähler polarizations and a quantum theory with genuine Kähler polarizations
L_{\Sigma}^{\tilde{{\rm P}}}
and complex structures
\tilde{J}_{\Sigma}
not only at the level of Hilbert spaces, but also of amplitudes. What is more, the theory with Hilbert spaces
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\Sigma}
and amplitudes
\tilde{\rho}_{M}
arises by applying precisely the original Kähler quantization prescription of Section
3
, but to the pulled back data.

We proceed to consider observables and their correlation functions. Pulling back correlation functions for observables
F
simply amounts to

\tilde{\rho}_{M}^{F}\big(\tilde{\psi}\big)=\rho_{M}^{F}\big(\tilde{U}_{\partial M}\big(\tilde{\psi}\big)\big).

We turn to the structure Theorem
3.1
for correlation functions in its entirety. In the factorization identity (
3.23
) of correlation functions of Weyl observables, we have already discussed the first factor, the amplitudes (
3.24
) and its replacement by relation (
7.15
). As for the third factor, the vacuum correlation function (
3.25
), there is no change as this does not depend on the choice of coherent state. However, it remains determined by the original polarization
L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
and not by the pulled back one
L_{\partial M}^{\tilde{{\rm P}}}
.
The second factor is of most interest. It takes the form

F\big(I_{\partial M}\big(\hat{\tilde{\xi}}\big)\big)
with
\hat{\tilde{\xi}}\coloneqq\xi^{\tilde{\mathrm{R}}}-\mathrm{i}\xi^{\tilde{\mathrm{I}}}
. In total we get

\tilde{\rho}^{F}_{M}\big(\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\xi}\big)=\tilde{\rho}_{M}\big(\tilde{\mathsf{k}}_{\xi}\big)F\big(I_{\partial M}\big(\hat{\tilde{\xi}}\big)\big)\rho^{F}_{M}(\mathsf{K}_{0}).

(7.16)

Unsurprisingly, this is structurally different from equation (
3.23
). That is, at the level of observables the actual theory (satisfying equation (
7.16
)) is not equivalent to the theory obtained by directly quantizing, according to Section
3
, the pulled back data (which would satisfy equation (
3.23
)).

The factorization equation (
7.16
), and in particular the second factor, also make it clear that a pull back map
I_{\Sigma}
is in general not suitable to achieve a semiclassical interpretation of coherent states. That is, the state
\mathsf{K}_{I_{\Sigma}(\xi)}^{\alpha}
is not in general a reasonable candidate for a semiclassical counterpart of the solution
\xi
. However, in Section
7.3
we already saw what a suitable counterpart might look like, compare equation (
7.7
).

A natural candidate for the identification map
I_{\Sigma}
is given by the projection operator
P_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
:
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
, that is,

P_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}(\phi+\alpha_{\Sigma}(\phi)).

(7.17)

Suppose that the restriction of
P_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
to the real subspace
L_{\Sigma}\subseteq L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
is a bijection
I_{\Sigma}\colon L_{\Sigma}\to L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
as required. Then, if the interior compatibility condition is satisfied for the real structure
\alpha
, it is automatically also satisfied for
I_{\Sigma}
.

7.7
A case of real polarizations

As shown in
[
8
]
, polarizations encoding vacua on different hypersurfaces and in different contexts in quantum field theory often decompose into a direct sum of a Kähler polarization and a real polarization. It is thus of particular interest to understand the latter in terms of
\alpha
-Kähler polarizations. To this end we shall assume that we are given what we will call a
positive-definite reflection map
.

Suppose that we have on the hypersurface
\Sigma
a real polarization, i.e., a pair of transversal complexified real Lagrangian subspaces
L^{\rm P}_{\Sigma}\subseteq L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
and
L^{\overline{\rm P}}_{\Sigma}\subseteq L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Moreover, say
\gamma_{\Sigma}\colon L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
is the complexification of a real-linear map
L_{\Sigma}\to L_{\Sigma}
with the following properties:

•

\gamma_{\Sigma}^{2}=\id
.

•

\omega_{\Sigma}(\gamma_{\Sigma}(\phi),\gamma_{\Sigma}(\eta))=-\omega_{\Sigma}(\phi,\eta)
.

•

\gamma_{\Sigma}
exchanges polarizations, i.e.,
\gamma_{\Sigma}\big(L^{\rm P}_{\Sigma}\big)=L^{\overline{\rm P}}_{\Sigma}
and
\gamma_{\Sigma}\big(L^{\overline{\rm P}}_{\Sigma}\big)=L^{\rm P}_{\Sigma}
.

We then say that
\gamma_{\Sigma}
is a
reflection map
on
\Sigma
.
By construction
\gamma_{\Sigma}(\mathrm{i}\phi)=\mathrm{i}\gamma_{\Sigma}(\phi)
and
\gamma_{\Sigma}\circ J_{\Sigma}=-J_{\Sigma}\circ\gamma_{\Sigma}
. Now define

\alpha_{\Sigma}(\phi)\coloneqq-\mathrm{i}\overline{\gamma(\phi)}\qquad\text{for}\quad\phi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}.

(7.18)

Then
\alpha_{\Sigma}
is a compatible real structure on
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Moreover, we assume the inner product (
7.2
) is positive-definite on
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
. More directly, we can write this condition as

\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\overline{\gamma_{\Sigma}(\phi)},\phi\big)>0\qquad\forall\phi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}\setminus\{0\}.

(7.19)

We then call
\gamma_{\Sigma}
a
positive-definite reflection map
and
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
is an
\alpha
-Kähler polarization with conjugate
L_{\Sigma}^{\overline{\rm P}}
. Moreover, in this situation the projector
P^{\alpha}_{\Sigma}\colon L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\to L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
, see equation (
7.17
), restricted to
L_{\Sigma}
becomes an isomorphism, as is easily verified. What is more, if we define the identification
I_{\Sigma}\colon L_{\Sigma}\to L_{\Sigma}^{\alpha}
of Section
7.6
to be

I_{\Sigma}=\sqrt{2}P_{\Sigma}^{\alpha},

we get, compare (
7.11
),

\tilde{\omega}_{\Sigma}(\phi,\eta)=\omega_{\Sigma}(\phi,\eta).

That is, the pulled back symplectic form is identical to the original symplectic form. In particular, as long as no observables are considered, this means that the pulled back theory of Section
7.6
defined in this way differs from the original theory merely by replacing the real polarizations
L_{\Sigma}^{\rm P}
with Kähler polarizations given in terms of the complex structures
\tilde{J}_{\Sigma}
given by (
7.12
).

We proceed to consider a more concrete way to construct positive-definite reflection maps. To this end we suppose that we have a decomposition of the space
L_{\Sigma}
of germs of solutions on
\Sigma
in terms of a direct sum of (real) Lagrangian subspaces
L_{\Sigma}=N_{\Sigma}\oplus M_{\Sigma}
. These need not coincide with polarizations determining the vacuum. For intuition, we shall think of
N_{\Sigma}
as encoding “positions” (field values) and of
M_{\Sigma}
as encoding “momenta” (normal derivatives). There is an important class of examples where this designation is indeed meaningful. We now define

\gamma_{\Sigma}(n+m)\coloneqq n-m,\qquad\forall n\in N_{\Sigma},\quad\forall m\in M_{\Sigma}.

(7.20)

Intuitively speaking, what this map does is it inverts field derivatives on the hypersurface. That is, it “reflects” initial data. We shall assume that it also interchanges the polarizations determining the vacuum on the two sides of
\Sigma
, i.e.,
L^{\rm P}_{\Sigma}
and
L^{\overline{\rm P}}_{\Sigma}
. It is then easy to check that
\gamma_{\Sigma}
is a reflection map according to our previous definition.

Even though Klein–Gordon theory does not require a reflection map as it admits a perfectly fine Kähler quantization we shall consider it here to illustrate the notion of reflection. On an equal-time hypersurface at time
t
the subspaces
N_{t}
and
M_{t}
of
L_{t}
are given by

\displaystyle M_{t}=\big\{\phi\in L_{t}^{\mathbb{C}}\colon\phi^{\rm a}(k)=-{\rm e}^{2\mathrm{i}Et}\overline{\phi^{\rm b}(-k)}\,\forall k\big\},

\displaystyle N_{t}=\big\{\phi\in L_{t}^{\mathbb{C}}\colon\phi^{\rm a}(k)={\rm e}^{2\mathrm{i}Et}\overline{\phi^{\rm b}(-k)}\,\forall k\big\}.

(7.21)

With this we obtain

(\gamma_{t}(\phi))^{\rm a}(k)={\rm e}^{2\mathrm{i}Et}\overline{\phi^{\rm b}(-k)},\qquad(\gamma_{t}(\phi))^{\rm b}(k)={\rm e}^{2\mathrm{i}Et}\overline{\phi^{\rm a}(-k)}.

(7.22)

Viewing elements of
L_{t}^{\mathbb{C}}
as global solutions this is in spacetime terms,

(\gamma_{t}(\phi))(t^{\prime},x)=\phi(2t-t^{\prime},x).

(7.23)

That is, we obtain precisely a reflection of the solution on the hyperplane at time
t
. It is also straightforward to check that
\gamma_{t}
interchanges the subspaces
L_{t}^{+}
and
L_{t}^{-}
of negative and positive energy solutions determining the vacuum, compare (
3.21
). That is,
\gamma_{t}
is a reflection map in the sense of our definition. On the other hand,
\gamma_{t}
is not positive-definite in the sense of (
7.19
). In fact, the would-be inner product defined by
\gamma_{t}
is indefinite. This is not surprising given that
\gamma
is designed for vacua given by real polarizations rather than Kähler polarizations. In the following Section
8
we exhibit a genuine example of a positive-definite reflection map in a context motivating the developments of the present section.

8
Reflection positivity and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
-Kähler quantization

The Euclidean approach to quantum field theory
[
28
,
52
]
is based on relating quantum field theoretic correlation functions in Minkowski space to corresponding correlation functions in Euclidean space via a version of
Wick rotation
. The
Euclidean correlation functions
, also known as
Schwinger functions
, have better analytic properties than their Lorentzian counterparts and can be naturally related to a statistical path integral, which is mathematically more tractable than the Feynman path integral. Osterwalder and Schrader
[
44
]
have given conditions for a family of Schwinger functions to correspond to a quantum field theory in the sense of the
Wightman axioms

[
51
]
. One of these conditions is
reflection positivity
and it can be understood to correspond to a realness condition for Lorentzian correlators or a Hermiticity condition for operators. It is also used to construct a Hilbert space directly from the Schwinger functions
[
43
]
. This construction is very different from the standard quantization constructions of quantum (field) theory which are always directly or indirectly tied to a Kähler polarization. In this case there is no Kähler polarization. This provided crucial inspiration for the construction presented in Section
7
based on the notion of
\alpha
-Kähler polarizations. The purpose of the present section is to elucidate this by showing (in the simplest possible setting) how the Hilbert space construction based on reflection positivity is recovered as a special case of what we shall call
\beta
-Kähler quantization, a Euclidean counterpart of
\alpha
-Kähler quantization.

8.1
Statistical path integral

Since it is of central interest in the Euclidean approach to field theory, we consider a replacement of the Feynman path integral with a
statistical path integral
as it appears for example in classical statistical field theory. That is, we replace the path integral formula (
2.4
) with a statistical integral defined as

\sigma^{F}_{M}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)=\int_{K_{M}}\mathcal{D}\phi\,F(\phi)\exp(-S(\phi)).

It is straightforward to accommodate for this change throughout the framework presented in this work. (Of course, the straightforward interpretation of the constructed mathematical objects in terms of a quantum theory is then lost.) We shall not develop this in detail, but focus on a few essential structures. We start with the analogue of formula (
2.6
) for Weyl observables. Consider a linear observable
D
, and its negative exponentiation
G=\exp(-D)
and let
\eta\in A_{M}^{D}\cap L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
. Thus,

\sigma_{M}^{G}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{2}D(\eta)\bigg).

(8.1)

Replacing
G
with the usual Weyl observable
F=\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
yields

\sigma_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}D(\eta)\bigg).

Note that to arrive at this expression we have to replace on the right-hand side of (
8.1
)
D
with
-\mathrm{i}D
as well as
\eta
with
-\mathrm{i}\eta
. We can read off from this that for arbitrary observables the relation between the Feynman path integral and the statistical path integral can be expressed as follows. Let
F
be an observable and define the observable
\tilde{F}
as

\tilde{F}(\phi)\coloneqq F\big(\sqrt{\mathrm{i}}\,\phi\big).

(8.2)

(For definiteness we choose to define the root of
\mathrm{i}
here as
\sqrt{\mathrm{i}}\coloneqq\exp(\mathrm{i}\pi/4)
.) Then,

\rho_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)=\sigma_{M}^{\tilde{F}}\big(W^{\rm P}\big).

(8.3)

In particular, the relations (
4.8
) of the Weyl algebra of slice observables are modified as follows,

\displaystyle G\star^{\rm S}F=\exp\big(\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi_{E},\xi_{D})\big)G\cdot F.

(8.4)

Proceeding to a “quantization” by analogy with the quantum theory, the role of the inner product (
1.6
) in
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
is taken by the expression

(\phi,\eta)^{\rm S}_{\Sigma}\coloneqq 4\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\overline{\phi},\eta\big).

(8.5)

This is clear from the modification that the vacuum correlation function (
5.16
) of a Weyl observable undergoes by transition to the statistical path integral. We get

v^{\rm S}_{\Sigma}(F)\coloneqq\sigma_{\hat{\Sigma}}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)=\exp\bigg({-}\frac{1}{4}\{\xi,\xi\}^{\rm S}_{\Sigma}\bigg).

Here we define

\{\xi,\xi\}^{\rm S}_{\Sigma}\coloneqq-\mathrm{i}\{\xi,\xi\}_{\Sigma}=4\omega\big(x^{\overline{\rm P}},y^{\rm P}\big).

A “quantization” with a construction of Hilbert spaces in the standard Kähler sense (Sections
3
and
5
) is clearly not possible as the sesquilinear form (
8.5
) is not even hermitian.
On the other hand, we can write down immediately the analogue of Theorem
6.1
.

Theorem 8.1
.

If
F
is the Weyl observable
\exp(\mathrm{i}D)
, then,

\displaystyle\sigma_{M}^{F}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{M}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)F\big({-}\mathrm{i}\xi^{\rm int}\big)\sigma_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big),\qquad\text{with}

\displaystyle\sigma_{M}\big(K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\omega_{\partial M}\big(\xi,\xi^{\rm int}\big)\big),

\displaystyle\sigma_{M}^{F}\big(W^{\rm P}\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}D(\eta)\bigg).

Here
\eta\in A_{M}^{D,\mathbb{C}}\cap L_{\partial M}^{\rm P}
.

With regard to the construction of Hilbert spaces it turns out that we can adapt the construction of Section
7
to the present setting and achieve the analog of a quantization in this way. Indeed, the considerations of Sections
7.1
and
7.2
carry over completely to the present setting with just one fundamental change. That is, we consider a real structure
\beta
on the vector space of complexified germs of solutions
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
. In contrast to the real structure
\alpha
introduced in Section
7.1
,
\beta
does not satisfy the compatibility condition (
7.1
), but rather the modified compatibility condition

\omega(\beta(x),\beta(y))=-\overline{\omega(x,y)}.

We call this condition
anti-compatibility
between
\beta
and
\omega
.
In this way the bilinear form

(\phi,\eta)^{\beta}\coloneqq 4\omega\left(\beta(\phi),\eta\right)

is not only non-degenerate and sesquilinear, but also hermitian. Thus, all steps described in Sections
7.1
and
7.2
can be performed. In particular, the quantization with the modified GNS construction succeeds with
\beta
determining the
*
-structure of the observable algebra in analogy to (
7.5
),

F^{\beta}(\phi)\coloneqq\overline{F\left(\beta(\phi)\right)}.

This leads to a notion of
\beta
-Kähler quantization in analogy to
\alpha
-Kähler quantization. In particular, the construction of Hilbert spaces and states can be preformed in accordance with Section
3.1
by replacing complex conjugation with the map
\beta
. In contrast to the
\alpha
-case, additionally the inner product
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is replaced by
\{\cdot,\cdot\}^{\rm S}
of (
8.5
) and equation (
3.2
) is replaced by

\{\phi,\eta\}^{\rm S}_{\Sigma}=-2\mathrm{i}\omega_{\Sigma}\big(\phi,J_{\Sigma}\eta\big)+2\omega_{\Sigma}(\phi,\eta).

We denote the Hilbert space constructed in this way by
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}^{\beta}
. Note that wave functions are defined on
L_{\Sigma}^{\beta}
, the subspace of
L_{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}
invariant under
\beta_{\Sigma}
. Similarly, coherent states are labeled by elements of
L_{\Sigma}^{\beta}
. In order to identify states in this space with the action of slice observables on the vacuum, the most straightforward consistent choice is to set (
6.2
)
(
with
\xi\in L_{\Sigma}^{\beta}\big)
and (
6.3
) as in the genuine quantum case.
We obtain instead of (
7.7
) the relation

K^{\rm P}_{\xi}\cong\exp\left(\omega_{\Sigma}(\xi,\tau)\right)\mathsf{k}^{\beta}_{\tau},

where
\tau=P^{-}_{\Sigma}(\xi)+\beta_{\Sigma}\big(P^{-}_{\Sigma}(\xi)\big)
. Otherwise, we proceed analogous to Section
7.3
. This determines the “quantization” completely.

As can be seen from the formulas, there is a close similarity between the
\alpha
-Kähler and
\beta
-Kähler quantizations. In fact, there is a straightforward one-to-one correspondence given by

\displaystyle\beta=\mathrm{i}\alpha,

(8.6)

which makes the relevant inner products equal,

(\phi,\eta)^{\beta}=(\phi,\eta)^{\alpha}.

This means in particular that given polarizations on a hypersurface
\Sigma
that admit a conventional Kähler quantization, the choice
\beta(\xi)\coloneqq\mathrm{i}\overline{\xi}
will yield a corresponding “quantization” based on the statistical path integral.

8.2
Wick rotated Klein–Gordon theory

We consider in the following a Wick rotation of the Klein–Gordon theory in Minkowski space as follows. We rotate the time coordinate
t
in the complex plane to an imaginary value. This can be considered as a substitution
t=-\mathrm{i}\tau
, where
\tau
is subsequently taken to be real. In this way the Klein–Gordon equation

\bigg(\partial_{t}^{2}-\sum_{i}\partial_{i}^{2}+m^{2}\bigg)\phi=0

turns into the elliptic partial differential equation

\bigg({-}\partial_{\tau}^{2}-\sum_{i}\partial_{i}^{2}+m^{2}\bigg)\phi=0.

We refer to the theory defined in this way as the
Euclidean theory
and consider it to live in 4-dimensional Euclidean space with coordinates
(\tau,x)
.

We recall the space
L_{0}^{\mathbb{C}}
of germs of complexified solutions of the Klein–Gordon equation on the hypersurface
t=0
from Section
3.2
, with the solutions parametrized in terms of plane waves (
3.18
). Applying the substitution
t=-\mathrm{i}\tau
we obtain

\phi(\tau,x)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}2E}\Big(\phi^{\rm a}(k){\rm e}^{-E\tau+\mathrm{i}kx}+\overline{\phi^{\rm b}(k)}{\rm e}^{E\tau-\mathrm{i}kx}\Big).

(8.7)

This is indeed a parametrization of the complexified solutions of the Euclidean theory near the
\tau=0
hypersurface in Euclidean space. We denote this space by
L_{0}^{{\rm E},\mathbb{C}}
. Via the substitution
t=-\mathrm{i}\tau
we have a canonical identification of the spaces
L_{0}^{\mathbb{C}}
and
L_{0}^{{\rm E},\mathbb{C}}
. Note, however, that while the elements of
L_{0}^{\mathbb{C}}
may be viewed as
global
solutions in Klein–Gordon theory, the elements of
L_{0}^{{\rm E},\mathbb{C}}
do not extend to sensible global solutions in Euclidean space due to their exponential behavior in the Euclidean time direction
\tau
. Moreover, the subspaces of real solutions
L_{0}\subseteq L_{0}^{\mathbb{C}}
in Klein–Gordon theory and
L_{0}^{{\rm E}}\subseteq L_{0}^{{\rm E},\mathbb{C}}
in the Euclidean theory are different. Whereas the reality condition in Klein–Gordon theory is
\phi^{\rm a}(k)=\phi^{\rm b}(k)
, the reality condition in the Euclidean theory is given by,
\phi^{\rm a}(k)=\overline{\phi^{\rm a}(-k)}
and
\phi^{\rm b}(k)=\overline{\phi^{\rm b}(-k)}
. Expressed in terms of Euclidean solutions the reality condition of the Klein–Gordon theory takes the form

\overline{\phi(\tau,x)}=\phi(-\tau,x).

An action for the Euclidean theory can also be obtained by Wick rotation from the Klein–Gordon theory, therefore inducing a symplectic form
\omega_{0}^{\rm E}
on
L_{0}^{\rm E}
,

\displaystyle\omega_{0}^{\rm E}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}x\,\big(\phi_{2}(\tau,x)(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{1})(\tau,x)-\phi_{1}(\tau,x)(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{2})(\tau,x)\big)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}2E}\Big(\phi_{2}^{\rm a}(k)\overline{\phi_{1}^{\rm b}(k)}-\phi_{1}^{\rm a}(k)\overline{\phi_{2}^{\rm b}(k)}\Big).
